Hamilton: Leclerc more aggressive racing me than he was Verstappen

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Lewis Hamilton says Ferrari's drivers "will have a sit down" with team boss Frederic Vasseur after the incident that cost them a likely victory in Mexico; watch Friday's practice sessions from the Sao Paulo Grand Prix at 3.30pm and 7pm live on Sky Sports F1

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Lewis Hamilton believes Ferrari team-mate Charles Leclerc races against him harder than he does other drivers on the Formula 1 grid.

Hamilton and Leclerc's clash on the opening lap of last Sunday's Mexico City Grand Prix cost Ferrari a likely victory, and potential one-two, with Max Verstappen instead ultimately profiting to claim victory for Red Bull.

Speaking after the race, Hamilton said the team needed "serious talks" to discuss the way forward to avoid a repeat in future, while also calling Leclerc's approach to such battles into question.

Some pundits have since suggested that Leclerc appears to race Hamilton particularly aggressively, comparing what happened in Mexico to the first corner at the Singapore Grand Prix last month when the Monegasque went head to head for the lead with Verstappen.

Asked on Thursday ahead of this weekend's Sao Paulo Grand Prix if he also saw it that way, Hamilton replied: "Yes.

"I see what you see. Singapore, he was fully [alongside Verstappen], wheel to wheel, fully on the inside for the lead at Turn 1 Singapore, and I think it was definitely different.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"But, I mean, if you look at the team-mates, it was the same with Sebastian [Vettel], it was the same with Carlos [Sainz]."

Hamilton and Leclerc have largely appeared to enjoy a friendly relationship off track during their first two years as team-mates, but there have been flash points in racing combat this season with the pair often in close company.

They included a costly incident on the first lap of the team's home race at Monza in September, when Hamilton was forced onto the gravel as the two cars went side by side at the first chicane in a clash his team-mate subsequently apologised for.

On Leclerc, Hamilton said: "He's been here a long time, is it eight years?

"He is very much embedded within the team, and I knew that it would always be a challenge.

"I've always respected that when I've approached the team. I've really worked hard to build a good relationship with Charles and bridge the gap and make sure that we're harmonious and we work hard together.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"I've always tried to be respectful, and so naturally it's not so easy having someone like me come into a team because I'm very outspoken and I push and push and push, push for so many changes, and I'm going to be pushing for more.

"And as long as I'm here, that's how it's going to be. I'm here to win, and to be able to win, you have to work with everyone around you.

"We're all rowing, trying to get everyone to row at the same pace and also the same strength. And you have to push, and I have to push, I have to be pushed also to elevate. We all can always elevate and be better.

"And I truly believe that this team can be fighting for the championship next year."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

F1 is swiftly back in action in Sao Paulo this week to complete the season's latest triple-header.

However, with team boss Frederic Vasseur having briefly flown back to the team's headquarters in Italy, and Leclerc returning home to Monaco for the birth of his first child, after last Sunday's race in Mexico City, Hamilton said they had not yet had the chance for in-depth discussions about the way forward.

The Briton was therefore the only Ferrari driver present to speak to the media at Interlagos on Thursday, with Leclerc not arriving at the track until Friday.

Hamilton said that "naturally I was frustrated and I'm still frustrated" about what happened in Mexico and "so the conversations need to be had, so we will have those when the time permits".

On whether anything was yet in place for this weekend, Hamilton said: "There isn't a plan at the moment because we haven't had a time to sit down, all face to face because Fred's been back in Maranello, and Charles has been back in Monaco, but we will have a sit down.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"I don't think everything's going to get squashed and sorted out in one meeting, but it is important what we build now will put us in the right position for next year.

"In some ways, I'm glad we've had that experience. Of course, I'd rather we had won and had a one-two but from that you can learn, and we can only really get stronger from that.

"In the background, we've got these incredible people that are working so hard in the background.

"We've been on a bit of the back foot on power this year, but the actual car has been phenomenal all the year, great reliability, and every day I go out on track, I'm always thinking of getting the absolute maximum for them.

"I don't always achieve it, and from that you learn. But I think with the years and experience, you understand the bigger picture when you're getting to work with such a large group of people, and that's, I would say, the privilege of the years that I've spent here and the teams that I've got to work in.

"I really do believe that next year we could win. We could be fighting for the world championship. That is the sole goal.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"Right now, in the background, my focus is on continuing to hold on to the performance we've picked up in the last few races in terms of execution, and then working on next year's car. I obviously can't do anything about the engine except for to just push them, and they'll continue to push in the background. I believe they are.

"With the car, it's steering it, making sure we're steering it in the right direction, and making sure we arrive next year on the right foot.

"Then Fred's job is to make sure that we are all running in the same direction. Of course, we're going to race hard but fair, and making sure that we get the maximum and the best result for the team."

Asked how he would approach discussions with Vasseur and Leclerc, Hamilton said: "I think it's about communication. It's about understanding.

"I haven't really tried to think what the meeting will be like, how it's going to go. You never ever know.

"But what I will do is I'll make sure I'm always just authentically me, and I won't back down from taking responsibility for any part that I play and any decisions that I make for the team, and just continue to push."

Hamilton said he would continue to push for the changes he believes will help Ferrari return to title-winning ways.

And while he denied claims some of his radio messages about the incident during the race in Mexico had been "calculated", he said he would not stop expressing his views openly and honestly.

"I don't really care how everyone feels about what I said," said Hamilton. "I wear my heart on my sleeve when I say how I feel in the moment.

"I think one thing that I would like to... I did see something that someone was saying, that it's calculated, the things that I'm saying. And I just want to combat that with the fact that when you're in the race, your heart rate is like 170, you're driving at 200 miles an hour, you don't have time to be calculating what you're saying and thinking what you're saying and what the media are going to write about.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"Usually, your frustration comes out towards the people in the team, particularly the bosses about decisions.

"And also, the stewards have always asked us to voice if there's an issue on track. For example, if you get held up, you get blocked, they ask you to voice it.

"An example is if you look in Mexico, one of the practice sessions, Hadjar backed off on the racing lines into Turn 1, I didn't say anything about it.

"If I had spoken and said, you know, like, throw my hands up, for example, they would have investigated and he would have had a penalty, because it was impeding.

"And so it is important to make sure you're open. Of course, there's sometimes you say things and maybe you might regret it afterwards. I don't. I've never apologised for being authentic and real.

"And I'm not going to be silenced. I've not been asked to not say anything, and that's just the way it is."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Hydrogen Derivatives Enable Cost-Effective Carbon Neutrality in China

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

We’re sharing this article early to provide faster access to peer-reviewed, accepted research. It is citable and carries a permanent DOI. This version is subject to further edits and will be replaced automatically by the final Version of Record. All legal disclaimers apply.

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change recommended 1.5°C carbon reduction target, requiring powerful enforcement from major economies. China, the largest CO2 emitter, is facing severe difficulties for both variable renewable accommodation and decarbonization of hard-to-abate sectors. Hydrogen, as a critical green energy carrier bridging renewable to end-users, faces significant obstacles in real-world deployment such as costly transportation, storage and fuel-cell adoption. Affordable and coordinated hydrogen deployment plan to accelerate the carbon-neutral transition remains ambiguous. Here, we present cost-effective carbon-neutral transition pathways for entire energy economy of China emphasizing the significant role of hydrogen and derivatives as green energy carriers, considering high-resolution renewable deployments, hourly power system operations in 29 provincial regions, over 46,000 rail/road/marine/pipeline transfer routing of hydrogen derivatives, process-level fuel adoption strategies in all end-use sectors. Results show that incorporating hydrogen and derivatives can reduce the overall transition cost by $ 10.7 trillion compared with renewable and electrification pathways, achieving negative carbon abatement costs ($-6.15/ton), eliminating external oil import expenses, equivalent to over 50% of China trade surplus (2023 level)1, significantly boosting employment and economic growth. Hydrogen derivative based transition pathway offers a vital direction for affordable and secure decarbonization for China and beyond under volatile international energy markets.

Sutton's predictions v Football Manager, the game

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

AI is leading the way in BBC Sport's prediction league table, but this week Chris Sutton has more than one machine to worry about.

His guest opponent for this weekend's fixtures is a computer game: Football Manager 27, the latest edition of the long-running management simulation series, which is released on 10 November.

The FM27 game engine has played out this weekend's matches and you can see its results, including goalscorers, below.

Miles Jacobson, the studio director of Sports Interactive, the company behind Football Manager, also joined in.

Do you agree with their predictions? You can pick your own below.

The most popular scoreline selected for each game is used in the scoreboards and tables at the bottom of this page.

A correct result (picking a win, draw or defeat) is worth 10 points. The exact score earns 40 points.

Want to make more predictions? You can play BBC Sport's new Match of the Day predictor game every week, featuring six fixtures from domestic and European competition.

One of the features in Football Manager 27 allows you to customise your manager's visual appearance, with wheelchair-using managers included for the first time

Among the new features in Football Manager 27 [FM27] is the expanded ability to tweak your manager's visual appearance, including an option that means wheelchair-using managers are represented in the game for the first time.

"It's something we are really proud of," Jacobson told BBC Sport. "Some might think it is only a small thing, but for wheelchair users it is an absolutely massive thing to be able to be represented in the game that they love.

"I am not a teary person, but I have to admit I had some tears in my eyes last weekend when I was watching some Instagram videos from our free trial of the game, of disabled people who just wanted to be seen, and represented.

"It seems really obvious to those of us who have been represented in games for years, but just giving people an opportunity to be represented, when they haven't been before, really does mean a lot to them. That has just been brilliant to see."

A more immersive world has been created for FM27, which includes a new training system called Tactical Effectiveness.

"It reflects what happens in real life when a new manager comes into a club and does something different - it takes players a while to get used to the new tactics," Jacobson said.

"So, when you are the manager coming into a club, you have to decide whether to spend more time training the players in a really complicated tactic - which is going to take them a long time to learn and which means less time training them on other things - or do you start off with the basics, and build from there?

"It's a little bit like what Nottingham Forest boss Oliver Glasner talked about after their performance in their defeat against Brentford last week, when he said he had been nice to his players, but now they have to listen and do what he is saying.

"I expect he is spending a lot of this week doing tactical effectiveness training in the background, or focusing on one part of it, because there are multiple elements."

Chris Sutton and Miles Jacobson were speaking to BBC Sport's Chris Bevan.

The AI predictions were generated using Microsoft Copilot Chat - we simply asked the tool to 'predict this weekend's Premier League scores'.

Like all the promoted teams, Coventry are having a go in games.

It has worked too, because they have picked up a few wins so far - they have three in the Premier League, as many as sixth-place Brentford.

It has been a strange season for the Sky Blues so far, because their performances have been a bit up and down. They can look very open, but those wins are the reward for their brave approach.

I don't see them getting anything here, however, on Frank Lampard's return to his former club.

Everton are not a free-scoring side, but I think they will have a bit too much nous for Coventry.

Miles' prediction: Both of these teams are in good form and this is probably the game I wrestled the most with before deciding what to go for. Thierno Barry has been a revelation for Everton this season and they showed against Newcastle that they can win without Jack Grealish, while Jack Rudoni has started to shine for Coventry at just the right time.

It's going to be a cracker of a game but I've gone with Everton to win, firstly because their defence is so strong at the moment, and Coventry might find them harder to break down than they did Sunderland, but also because David Moyes' side are playing with a lot of confidence now. 2-1

Tottenham always seem to do well against Leeds - they have only lost one of their past 15 meetings in the Premier League, going back almost 25 years.

I am a little bit of a believer that some teams always do well, or badly, against certain opposition or on certain grounds.

I had the same as a player when I had bogey teams or stadiums, as well as lucky ones. For example, while I always used to score against Everton at Goodison Park, I always had a stinker at Highfield Road, Coventry's old home, despite getting plenty of goals against them in home games for Blackburn.

So, I am definitely not going for Leeds to win this - but do I back Tottenham to win again and continue their good run?

Roberto de Zerbi's side have rocketed up the table on the back of three wins in a row, although they were helped out a lot by Jean-Philippe Mateta's red card in their victory over Crystal Palace last time out.

Tottenham are flying at the moment, and their confidence is up - we are seeing a bit of a Spurs swagger for the first time in a long while. So, while I don't think they will make it four wins in a row, I don't see them being beaten, either.

It's likely to be a very tight game. Leeds were a bit unlucky to lose to Bournemouth last week, but their lack of goals is becoming an issue.

Miles' prediction: Leeds had such a brilliant start to the season, while Spurs have turned the corner and they have got lots of players coming back to full fitness. I can't separate them. 1-1

Hull are scrapping for points and hanging on in there in a lot of games.

I get a lot of my predictions wrong for their games because I always go for them to lose, but I am not going to do anything different for them this week because of who they are playing.

Arsenal are just monsters, aren't they? They always seem to find a way and do enough to win games, even when they don't play well, and they will grind another result out here if they have to.

Miles' prediction: Hull have been so entertaining, and I just absolutely love watching them because they play with no fear whatsoever. At the start of the season you would have looked at this and thought Arsenal would hit them for four or five, but I think it will be much closer than that because you can never write off Ollie McBurnie.

I enjoy watching Arsenal too, by the way, in the same way that I enjoy watching chess. They are a beautiful team to watch tactically and it is just difficult for them when they attack at the moment because they have lost a lot of their width with Bukayo Saka out injured. They seem to be grinding out a lot of their results but I still enjoy watching them - I enjoy watching any team, for different reasons. One of the beautiful things about football is there is no one set way to play it.

Arsenal often go 1-0 up and shut up shop, but they will have to do a bit more than that here, because Hull will keep going at them. 2-1

What are Newcastle? What type of team are they? I am not sure and that's why their manager, Matthias Jaissle, is getting a bit of stick from Magpies fans.

That's what is different with today's Premier League compared to my playing days, because we see it all the time now - even a new manager comes under pressure if they have a couple of bad results.

Newcastle have lost three of their past four league games and, in this day and age, that's enough for people to say the wheels are coming off.

That's why this game feels big for Jaissle. Win, and the Magpies head into the international break on a positive note. Lose it, however, and stand by for a lot of moaning and grumbling from their fans over the next couple of weeks.

That's what I think will happen, too. Fulham's results have picked up and so have their performances. They are at home here, and I am expecting them to be too slick and too well organised for Newcastle.

Miles' prediction: Fulham are playing a completely different formation under Alvaro Arbeloa this season and they have taken a long time to build it up.

Newcastle are definitely a project under Matthias Jaissle, too. I've just taken Yoane Wissa out of my Fantasy team so I am not expecting him to start scoring this week - I think they need the international break to sort out some of their manager's ideas. 0-0

Nottingham Forest just cannot score - they have managed only six league goals in their first nine games, the lowest total in the Premier League.

Their manager, Oliver Glasner, got a bit stroppy after their defeat by Brentford last time out, saying he will make changes or he will end up being sacked. He tried the carrot approach at the start of the season but his team didn't bite - so now he is trying the stick.

We might see a reaction here, and I do think Forest will score - Manchester City goalkeeper Gianluigi Donnarumma might even chuck one in for them, going off his recent poor form.

It certainly won't be easy for City and they have their own issues at the moment after back-to-back league defeats, but I am still expecting them to create enough chances to win.

Miles' prediction: Forest are another team who could do with the international break. Last season, Nikola Milenkovic and Murillo were two of the best centre-backs in the Premier League, but they look completely out of sorts at the moment. I can't see anything but a City win here. 0-3

I just can't see past Brighton here - they are superbly organised, carry a threat going forward and defend well. Their bench is strong as well.

Brentford are also going well and are capable of causing them a problem or two - but, after watching Brighton beat Manchester City at Etihad Stadium last week, I am backing Fabian Hurzeler's side here.

I always think the Bees have got a goal in them, so I am going to say they will score, but the Seagulls are smart enough to come out on top.

People seem to be unsure whether Brighton are really in the title race or not, but they are certainly in the mix for second place if Arsenal are the team we all think are going to win it.

Miles' prediction: What a revelation Brighton have been. They are just beautiful to watch and, again, they play with no fear. What they are doing on and off the pitch is just fantastic, but then you could say the same about Brentford. This will be a brilliant game and I wish I could watch it live. 2-2

Crystal Palace boss Pierre Sage was not helped by Mateta's sending-off in their defeat by Tottenham and the Eagles striker will miss this game after they failed with their appeal to overturn the decision.

Whether you thought that was a red card or not, you feel for Sage because he is coming under a lot of pressure and a lot of Palace's problems are not his fault - he took charge at a difficult time.

Liverpool have issues too, and they come into this game off the back of a lousy performance and result against Fenerbahce on Wednesday.

Aston Villa hit the Turkish side for six a couple of weeks ago, but Liverpool struggled to keep them quiet and Palace might feel they can pick the Reds off in the same way.

Liverpool won't change the way they play under Andoni Iraola, so they will try to be aggressive with their press but are likely to be left open again if Palace get past it.

I don't see that happening this time, though, mostly because Palace have been so poor.

Liverpool were leaky against Fenerbahce, but I am going for them to keep a clean sheet here and get a sneaky away win, with Alexander Isak and Bradley Barcola getting their goals.

Miles' prediction: I have mates who work at Palace and I know they probably won't talk to me for a week after this prediction, but I am going with Liverpool here. Mateta's suspension is huge for the Eagles, and Ismaila Sarr is still out injured. 0-2

Bournemouth have turned into one of the Premier League's form teams.

I thought they would run out of steam against Leeds last week but I was wrong, and they have shown they have got a bit of depth in their squad.

I've been surprised by Ipswich too, and they were unlucky not to beat Hull last time out. The Tractor Boys have got a bit of spirit about them, which makes me think they will get something from this game.

Bournemouth are a front-foot team under Marco Rose, who is a very attack-minded manager, but Ipswich will have a go too. I can see them getting a point.

Miles' prediction: I have been really impressed by Ipswich in the past few weeks and Julio Enciso has been doing really well for them - but Bournemouth are absolutely flying. What a season they are having. 0-2

I keep hearing how Sunderland boss Regis le Bris is under pressure. Really? After finishing seventh and getting a promoted side into Europe last season, it seems crazy that his job is at risk now.

Still, Le Bris has plenty of problems to solve while his squad is being stretched by the Europa League - before this game they play Dinamo Zagreb on Thursday.

The Black Cats won this fixture on the final day of the last campaign, but their home record is nothing like as strong this time around.

They are also conceding an average of almost two goals a game in the Premier League, compared to 2025-26 when only three teams had a better defensive record.

Add that to their problems scoring - Forest are the only team with fewer goals so far - and you can see why they are bottom of the table.

I am still not sure how to judge Chelsea yet - yes, they beat Manchester United last week, but everyone beats Manchester United - but I just don't see the Black Cats keeping them out.

Miles' prediction: Sunderland are struggling with having European football and while I think they will turn things around, it won't be this week. Chelsea's start to the new season has not been ideal and Joao Pedro being out injured is a huge loss to them - but I am still backing them here. 1-2

There were great scenes at Old Trafford on Tuesday with Manchester United coming back from 2-0 down to beat Roma, who had to field their third-choice goalkeeper because of injuries.

That ended up being a really good result, but there are so many things about United that are just not convincing at the moment - as a club they are trying to convey this picture of togetherness and it is just not there for me.

Beating Roma was a great comeback, of course, but you still feel United are not far from another collapse, especially against an Aston Villa team who are so well structured, and capable of punishing any weakness.

Villa boss Unai Emery has been talked about as a potential successor to Michael Carrick at Old Trafford, but he is too good for United as far as I can see.

United will score, but Villa are going to win this. Nicolas Jackson will bag a couple and we might see more tears from Bruno Fernandes at the final whistle.

Miles' prediction: I am probably going to get pelters from United fans from this, including some of my team members at Sports Interactive. One of them, who is a season-ticket holder, told me he wasn't going to this game, and therefore United will probably win... but I just can't see that happening.

I really feel for Michael Carrick because he did such a brilliant job last season and I hope he manages to turn things around, but Villa are looking really good at the moment. 1-2

Chris got four correct results, including two exact scores, from the 10 games in week nine, giving him a total of 100 points.

He beat his guest, Bad Day At The Office star John Hannah, who got four correct results with no exact scores, for a tally of 40 points.

And AI's four-week winning run is over, after it got five correct results and one exact score to end up on 80 points.

But the weekly win was claimed by the collective efforts of the BBC readers. They managed five correct results, including two exact scores, giving them 110 points.

There was only one game where everyone picked the right outcome - Chelsea's 2-0 win over Manchester United.

Chris, John and AI all backed the Blues to win, just by the wrong score, but a lot of you got that part spot on too.

From more than 40,000 predictions by the BBC readers, that was the most popular scoreline selected, with almost 10,000 of you getting it exactly right.

Forest need balance between Woodstock and military - Glasner

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nottingham Forest's defeat at Brentford was their sixth defeat in 10 games in all competitions this season

Oliver Glasner insists his struggling Nottingham Forest side cannot survive on "flower power" as he tries to find a combination of trust and respect from his squad.

Forest are 19th in the Premier League and winless and goalless at home in the top flight.

Last weekend's 2-0 defeat at Brentford was a fourth defeat in five games as Glasner warned there would be no more compromises after his summer arrival, and he has underlined his standards.

"These are my two values I will never negotiate about, this is trust and respect," he said.

"It can't be one, so if it's just respect, I would say that's military. Then, after a few months of the national service, you are pleased when you can leave.

"When it's just trust, it's a flower power, just flying. Everybody can do what he wants and it's nice and great - Woodstock - but it's not high-performance Woodstock.

"Military is [high performance], to be honest, because otherwise this huge organisation couldn't work, and I felt we had too much Woodstock.

"We need a good balance between Woodstock, having fun, but also military, high performance. That's the most important thing, high performance, because that's what we get paid for, that's our job. If we have this - fly."

When asked by BBC Sport if he should have told his players sooner Glasner said: "I always ask myself this question - should I have done it earlier?

"Of course, maybe two or three times I felt it's time and then I thought 'no, come on Oliver, be patient and maybe things change'.

"Then it seemed to be the same game [Brentford] like the last ones, so I felt I have to say it now because otherwise things won't change."

Glasner is not under immediate pressure and remains in close communication with the Forest hierarchy, with stability desired after four managers last season.

The 52-year-old has made a positive impression with his work and methods, even if there have been some obvious grumbles from players who are not playing, and there remains a desire to stay calm.

"I'm always in contact with the ownership," Glasner said. "It's not always Mr [Evangelos] Marinakis because he has other stuff to do, but there is his son [Miltiadis], George [Syrianos, chief football officer] and Pedro [Ferreira, technical director] here at the training ground, and Theodore [Giannikos] the CEO.

"There's always communication - and that's a good thing, because everybody understands better why I am how I am. I never say what's best for Oliver Glasner, because I don't feel important. I will always say what I believe is best for Nottingham Forest, for the club.

"I don't like to accept things if I feel they are wrong. My addiction is to succeed with Nottingham Forest and, right now, I have failed. Then I get really upset with myself because I want to meet the expectations of the players, owner and the fans.

"Right now, I've failed and that's why I blame myself most."

England check on Palmer - what are Tuchel's other big choices?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Thomas Tuchel will name his next England squad on Friday

England have made medical checks on Cole Palmer as Thomas Tuchel considers whether to select the Chelsea forward in his next squad.

The 24-year-old missed all four games during the previous international window with a minor injury.

He completed 90 minutes for Chelsea against Brentford before those matches, and started for the Blues in the first game afterwards.

Tuchel, who names his squad to face Croatia and Spain on Friday, said at the time that Palmer was "playing with discomfort and pain" but was "missing too many opportunities" with England.

Whether he is recalled will be a key theme on Friday. While it is normal for England medical staff to check on players under consideration, it also underlines that Palmer remains on Tuchel's radar despite his stark comments in September.

In addition to deciding whether to select Palmer, head coach Tuchel has a number of other dilemmas to resolve.

Bukayo Saka's injury absence opens the door to another attacker, with his Arsenal team-mate Noni Madueke pushing for a recall following a string of impressive performances.

Manchester United forward Marcus Rashford has not played since mid-October and is not fit for selection, while Chelsea defender Trevoh Chalobah is a major injury doubt.

Defenders Ben White and Djed Spence are pushing for recalls after missing out on the previous squad through injury.

The likes of Harry Kane, Jude Bellingham, Declan Rice and many others are now stonewall selections - but the intrigue is around the remaining few spots still seemingly up for grabs.

Will some old faces earn a recall or will there be new faces soon pulling on the England shirt? BBC Sport runs down some of the potential candidates for selection.

The 19-year-old Fulham midfielder has already been close to a senior England debut.

King travelled to the US as part of the preparation squad before the World Cup, along with Alex Scott, Ethan Nwaneri and Rio Ngumoha.

Scott and Ngumoha have already made their debuts for the senior side and Nwaneri is currently on loan at Borussia Dortmund.

Tuchel said in his last news conference how King is close to a senior call-up and the midfielder has impressed for England Under-21s too.

The biggest challenge for King will be finding a chance to shine in his favoured number 10 position with Bellingham being England's best midfielder and the position being one of real strength for the Three Lions.

Will Lankshear has been prolific for Middlesbrough this season

There is a lack of number nines in the English game and the job of being Harry Kane's understudy is harder than it sounds.

The England captain has just won the Ballon d'Or, likes to play almost every minute for his country and is the captain, record goalscorer and arguably the best player in Tuchel's side.

Dominic Calvert-Lewin has been selected in the squads before and since the World Cup but, despite doing well for Leeds, it has not quite clicked for him under Tuchel and he was given just 15 minutes in two substitute appearances in the last window.

Tuchel has started to use Bellingham as the back-up to Kane, but he could opt for more of a prospect in his squad in this shorter window and turn to Lankshear.

The 21-year-old striker, who like Kane started at Tottenham, was sold to Middlesbrough this summer for Â£20m and has scored 15 goals in all competitions so far this campaign.

He has scored 13 goals in as many games in the Championship, helping Middlesbrough to the top of the division.

He only made his debut for the England Under-21s during the last international window and scored in that game so it would be quite a rise for a player who is not in the Premier League.

Tuchel is aware of Lankshear and with a lack of number nines across the age groups it could be an interesting option for a manager who is not afraid of making big calls.

He did miss Middlesbrough's loss to Wrexham on Wednesday with a tweaked hamstring but could return in time for Saturday.

The Everton number 10 has turned down call-ups from the Republic of Ireland as he focuses on his dream of trying to represent the Three Lions.

He has been one of the Toffees' standout performers having scored two goals and registered two assists in all competitions, helping Everton to seventh in the league.

Dewsbury-Hall, 28, is a clever technician with an eye for a pass and his ability on the ball would likely suit international football.

But team-mate James Garner, who Tuchel called a mini-Valverde, was called up before Dewsbury-Hall when there were dropouts in the last international break with that showing perhaps he has more to do to get on Tuchel's radar.

Gibbs-White was probably the unluckiest player to be left out of the initial squad last time around when Tuchel got his players together in September.

But the Nottingham Forest midfielder was called up as a late replacement after a number of high profile pull-outs and left a family holiday to join up with the Three Lions squad.

Tuchel values that kind of dedication and referenced the willingness of Gibbs-White, 26, to join up.

The Forest captain featured during every game of the last break although he did not start a game.

But with Forest struggling at the bottom end of the Premier League table - and competing against the likes of Bellingham, Phil Foden, Palmer and Eberechi Eze - the midfielder could miss out again.

A must pick or unreliable? Tuchel's Palmer dilemma

White has been unlucky since he ended his international hiatus to return in March.

The Arsenal defender missed the World Cup with a knee injury that forced him to also miss the Champions League final.

White then missed the first England squad of this campaign with injury, with Tuchel saying the 29-year-old would have been called up if he had been fit, citing his partnership with Saka as potentially good for the Three Lions.

But White has been available for all but one of Arsenal's games this season and would add a quality defensive option at right-back if selected, especially with Reece James missing through injury.

Henderson has not been in the England set-up since the World Cup, where he fell over an advertising hoarding following the win over Mexico in the Azteca Stadium and broke his arm.

Many would have thought the 36-year-old's international career was over but Henderson, then at Brentford, made a move to Chelsea as they looked to add experience to their young squad.

After recovering from the arm injury, Henderson has surprised some to become an important part of the Chelsea midfield, starting five games in a row and scoring twice in a win over Bournemouth, before he missed the Blues' win over Manchester United with injury.

Henderson is a popular figure, respected by staff and players and is again performing at the top level.

A bitter end for Ronaldo - the icon who transformed Portugal

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Cristiano Ronaldo's old rival Leo Messi left Argentina less than a month ago with a nation applauding him.

The Portugal legend has gone another way, and it was always going to be the case.

The 41-year-old announced his international retirement after 23 years but also after a fallout with coach Jorge Jesus which was resolved with a sanction of 30 days, that had already been served, and a fine of 255 euros.

It was the punishment imposed on the Portuguese striker for leaving the team's training camp without permission. Jesus kept him on the bench for one match and said he did not plan to start him in the next, so Ronaldo went home.

His last appearance came on 24 September against Wales. He ends his international career with 146 goals in 234 games.

A message went around Portugal with a big thank you for everything, calling Ronaldo a global ambassador and an inspiration to millions. Even so, the way it ended leaves a bit of a bitter taste.

Was he going to be named in the next squad? Possibly, as Jesus said the door was open and the decision was Ronaldo's. What is clear is that he was no longer an automatic starter, and Cristiano has never handled that well.

So is this another hissy fit? Partly, yes. He reacted the way he always has when a coach questions his place. But the label hides how much he adapted over the years.

Such a pattern appears with almost every man who sat on the Portuguese bench - there were clashes, but also communion with managers and team-mates that help explain this ending.

Six tournaments, 11 goals - Ronaldo's World Cup legacy

Ronaldo's most iconic moments as he retires from international football

What legacy does Cristiano Ronaldo leave on the international stage?

When a young Ronaldo first joined the squad, he arrived at camp with his baseball cap on backwards, enormous sunglasses and headphones turned up so loud they could be heard from a distance.

He would not take the cap off to eat among veterans like Luis Figo and Rui Costa. Luiz Felipe Scolari saw an extraordinary player who was immature and obsessed with showing off. He reprimanded him in public, then told him privately that if he changed his attitude, he would one day captain Portugal.

The first great disappointment came early, and it came at home. At 19, Ronaldo played in the final of Euro 2004 at the Estadio da Luz in Lisbon, in front of a country that had convinced itself the trophy was already on its way.

A single headed goal by Angelos Charisteas was enough for unfancied Greece to win it by surprise. When the final whistle went, Ronaldo could not hold back the tears, and the image of the teenager crying on his own pitch travelled everywhere.

It was a painful way to learn what a final costs, and it left him with a hunger that never really went away.

Figo owned the number 7 at the time, so Ronaldo had to have 17. Figo helped him settle but had no intention of making it easy. When he left the national team in 2006, the shirt and the team became Ronaldo's.

In September 2005, before a qualifier in Russia, Scolari and Figo went to his room to tell him his father had died. Scolari offered to send him home. Ronaldo chose to play, asked his team-mates to behave as normal, and spent time in the dressing room doing ball tricks.

That was his way to cope with the expected but still hugely hurtful passing of his alcoholic dad. He later called Scolari "father". That way the national team had become the place that held him together.

Things went sour with Carlos Queiroz. After the 2010 World Cup exit against Spain, asked to explain a poor performance, Ronaldo told journalists to ask the coach. His influence in the camp had grown so large it changed routines and habits, which explains why the coach felt undermined.

The sociologist Joao Nuno Coelho offers the best lens on how Portugal sees him. In his analysis, the national team became a representation of Portuguese identity and of the country's need to be seen internationally.

In years of economic hardship and a crisis of confidence, a team that won admiration abroad was important beyond football.

Ronaldo stood at the centre of that need and created contradictory feelings. Some Portuguese questioned his individualism and his ego, and not everyone saw themselves in his personality.

Coelho explains criticising him became more difficult as time went by. The country wanted to be proud of him and sometimes felt uneasy at the same time.

The former international Jorge Andrade told me some supporters preferred figures like Figo, Eusebio, Paulo Futre or Rui Costa. Even so, Portugal was proud to have him. And with the titles, the doubts eased.

Under Fernando Santos in 2016, he changed. He accepted playing as a centre forward next to Nani - he had never taken a coach's instructions so naturally.

After the opening draw with Iceland, he told Nani in private that he trusted Santos, and the dressing room began to see a real captain. After missing a penalty against Austria, he barely slept. Santos woke up and found him already awake.

At the training camp in Marcoussis, he took a fan's megaphone and told supporters Portugal had won nothing yet. He was enjoying the leadership role.

In the final in Paris, he left the pitch in tears, injured. He had not had the best tournament, with a muscle complaint that did not let him perform at his best.

Nani took the armband. At half-time Ronaldo pulled himself together to encourage his team-mates, and before extra time, still limping, he told Eder he would score.

But the old instinct never fully disappeared. During extra-time, Ronaldo stood in the technical area giving orders until the fourth official sent him back. Other coaches I consulted found it excessive.

His greatest strength, the need to influence every result, also made limits hard to accept.

When he debuted in 2003, Portugal was still searching for its first major title. Some players mark an era, and others change a national team for good. He belongs to the second group.

That night in July 2016 gave Portugal its first European Championship.

Three years later he scored a hat-trick against Switzerland to put them into the first Nations League final, which Portugal won. In 2025 he lifted a second Nations League at an age when most players have retired.

No one has scored more goals for a national side, and no one has pulled on the Portugal shirt more times.

Under his influence, Portugal stopped being a gifted team and became one that arrives at every tournament expecting to contend, while a whole generation of players came through a dressing room where he decided what the standard was.

Coelho's point holds here as well: a small country found a way to be noticed, and for two decades he was its face.

After 2016 the question around him changed. Fewer asked whether he was selfish, and more asked whether Portugal depended on him too much.

Ronaldo scored 146 goals and won 234 caps for Portugal

In the World Cup in 2018, he scored three against Spain, including a free-kick after 45 attempts in major tournaments, and the team still went out to Uruguay.

After a difficult 2026 World Cup, much was made of him still starting up front when in fact statistically that was his best period.

Under Roberto Martinez, with 28 goals in 37 games, 0.756 per match, improves the 0.624 with Santos.

The federation has announced a special tribute to honour his contribution, and that is necessary as the exit has not left fans room for a celebration they want to be part of.

Not only they grew up with him, he took them to places they had not yet been.

Opportunistically Detecting Signs of Hypertension on a Consumer Smartwatch

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

We’re sharing this article early to provide faster access to peer-reviewed, accepted research. It is citable and carries a permanent DOI. This version is subject to further edits and will be replaced automatically by the final Version of Record. All legal disclaimers apply.

Hypertension is a silent killer, with over half of affected adults unaware of their condition1,2. This lack of awareness is a major concern, as early intervention is critical for preventing major adverse cardiovascular events3,4. Here we show that applying artificial intelligence (AI) to multimodal waveforms (photoplethysmography and accelerometry) recorded using a widely available consumer smartwatch (AI-PPG-ACC-HTN), without any cuff calibration, can detect hypertension with accuracy levels comparable to traditional cuffed blood pressure (BP) devices in the existing clinical framework. We validated AI-PPG-ACC-HTN in a prospective, multicenter study of 196 diverse participants free from known cardiovascular disease and antihypertensive medication against gold-standard 24-hour ambulatory BP monitoring. Over seven days of real-world monitoring, AI-PPG-ACC-HTN detected hypertension with a sensitivity of 65.8% (95% CI, 54.0%-76.3%), specificity of 90.0% (95% CI, 83.2%-94.7%), and positive predictive value (PPV) of 80.6% (95% CI, 68.6%-89.6%). In comparison, initial office BP screening achieved a sensitivity of 55.3% (95% CI, 43.4%-66.7%), specificity of 90.0% (95% CI, 83.2%-94.7%) and PPV of 77.8% (95% CI, 64.4%-88.0%). These results highlight an opportunity for consumer smartwatches to facilitate population-level opportunistic hypertension screening by prompting at-risk individuals toward clinical assessment and confirmatory diagnosis using established cuff-based methodologies, offering an accessible tool to address this major public health challenge.

Scientists discover that a clogged filter could revolutionize oil refining

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Researchers have developed a surprisingly simple way to separate crude oil without heating it, potentially transforming one of the world's most energy-intensive industrial processes. The breakthrough uses an inexpensive membrane that could significantly reduce the energy required for oil refining while lowering operating costs and carbon emissions.

The international research effort was led by Professor Dong-Yeun Koh at the Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology, known as KAIST, in collaboration with Professor Ryan Lively's research group at Georgia Tech. KAIST(President Kwang Hyung Lee) announced that the team had successfully demonstrated a membrane capable of separating crude oil at room temperature. The findings were published in Nature, one of the world's leading scientific journals.

Crude oil is essential for far more than gasoline and other transportation fuels. It also supplies the raw materials used to manufacture plastics, packaging, clothing, and numerous everyday products. Because refining costs affect the prices of these materials, making the process more efficient could have widespread economic and environmental benefits.

Why Traditional Crude Oil Refining Requires So Much Energy

For more than a century, oil refineries have relied heavily on distillation to separate crude oil into useful components. The process involves heating oil to temperatures above 350 °C, turning portions of it into vapor, and then cooling those vapors to collect different fractions.

This approach requires an enormous amount of energy. Worldwide, crude oil distillation consumes approximately 1,100 terawatt-hours (TWh) annually. That is comparable to the combined yearly electricity production of roughly 130 continuously operating nuclear power plants, each with a capacity of about one gigawatt.

The massive energy demand makes distillation a major contributor to both fuel consumption and greenhouse gas emissions within the refining industry. Meanwhile, growing economic pressures across global petrochemical markets have increased interest in technologies that can separate oil more efficiently.

Membranes have emerged as a promising alternative because they can separate substances without requiring the extreme temperatures used in conventional distillation. However, previous designs have faced significant obstacles.

Traditionally, researchers have assumed that effective molecular separation requires a membrane covered with an extremely thin, specialized coating. Although these coatings can provide the necessary precision, they are expensive to produce and can develop defects when manufactured on a large scale. These limitations have made widespread industrial adoption difficult.

A Surprising Discovery Turns Clogged Filters Into an Advantage

The KAIST and Georgia Tech researchers approached the problem differently. Rather than adding a complicated coating, they used a bare membrane made from porous polyacrylonitrile (PAN), an inexpensive, chemically stable polymer already commonly used as a supporting material in industrial filtration systems.

What happened when crude oil passed through the membrane was unexpected.

As the oil flowed through its pores, heavier hydrocarbon molecules accumulated along the inner walls. This buildup gradually reduced the size of the openings, producing extremely narrow channels measuring less than 2 nanometers across.

In effect, the crude oil helped create the microscopic pathways needed to separate its own components.

These naturally formed channels allowed lighter oil fractions, including naphtha, gasoline, and kerosene, to pass through quickly while preventing heavier substances from traveling through the membrane as easily.

The discovery was especially surprising because the buildup of material inside membrane pores, known as fouling, is normally considered a serious problem. Fouling typically reduces filtration performance and requires cleaning or replacement. In this case, however, the same phenomenon made the membrane highly effective at separating different molecules.

The results were impressive. The uncoated PAN membrane achieved crude oil permeation rates approximately 23 times higher than those reported for previous leading crude oil separation membranes. It also continued operating with stable performance for 28 consecutive days.

Professor Ryan Lively (Georgia Tech) commented: "One of the key challenges facing membrane systems for crude oil separation was the low productivities of the membrane units -- the PAN membranes with their surprising separation mechanism -- dramatically increase the productivity of the membrane unit, to the point where industry should seriously consider adopting the technology."

New Membrane Could Cut Oil Refining Energy Use by Nearly a Third

One of the technology's biggest advantages is that refineries would not necessarily need to replace their existing equipment to use it.

The membrane could instead be installed as a modular filtration system that processes crude oil before it enters conventional distillation equipment. This would reduce the amount of work required during the heating and separation stages while allowing facilities to continue using much of their current infrastructure.

Computer simulations suggest that incorporating the membrane into the refining process could deliver substantial improvements. Energy consumption could fall by 31.6%, while carbon dioxide emissions could decrease by 37.6%. The same calculations projected a 20.7% reduction in cooling water consumption and a 36% decrease in operating costs.

The potential environmental benefits could be considerable if the technology were adopted widely.

Researchers estimate that implementing the system throughout Korea's refining and petrochemical industries could prevent approximately 10 million tonnes of greenhouse gas emissions each year. That is roughly equivalent to the annual emissions produced by four million vehicles powered by internal combustion engines.

These savings are projections based on process simulations rather than reductions already demonstrated across operating refineries. Nevertheless, the findings suggest that membrane filtration could become a practical way to improve the efficiency of existing facilities.

Although crude oil separation was the main focus of the research, the same technology could eventually be useful in several other industries.

One potential application involves treating pyrolysis oil produced when waste plastics are broken down for recycling. The membranes could also help recover solvents used in battery manufacturing, purify pharmaceutical compounds, and improve biofuel production.

The broader opportunity lies in using complex liquid mixtures to help form the tiny filtration channels needed to separate their components. This approach could provide a more affordable and versatile alternative to conventional membranes that require precisely manufactured surface coatings.

Professor Dong-Yeun Koh of KAIST said, "This study reveals a new scientific principle in which a membrane interacts with a complex mixture and spontaneously forms its own separation channels. Working with real crude oil supplied by HD Hyundai Oilbank allowed us to validate the technology under conditions relevant to industrial operation."

The use of actual crude oil was an important part of the research because it allowed the team to examine how the membrane performed with the complicated mixture of hydrocarbons found in industrial feedstocks.

Before the technology can be deployed more widely, the researchers plan to improve membrane production at larger scales and establish its reliability during extended operation.

Professor Jae W. Lee of KAIST, a co-corresponding author of the study, added, "By advancing large-area membrane modularization and long-term operational reliability, we hope to broaden the adoption of membrane-based processes throughout the refining and petrochemical industries."

Dr. Jihoon Choi and Dr. Hyeokjun Seo of KAIST, the study's co-first authors, said, "Our goal is to precisely control this spontaneous pore-constriction phenomenon and develop it into a membrane platform applicable to the entire refining process. We also aim to expand the technology to plastic recycling, biofuel purification, and other sustainable chemical processes that support carbon neutrality."

The research received support from Korea's Ministry of Science and ICT through the Basic Research Program for Outstanding Early-Career Researchers and the Engineering Research Center (ERC) Program.

Materials provided by The Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology (KAIST). Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Does the Oleshky ceasefire signal progress to come in Ukraine?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Hundreds of civilians were evacuated from the city of Oleshky after Russia and Ukraine agreed to a US-brokered ceasefire. Some observers have expressed optimism that more such humanitarian missions might follow.

Nearly 800 residents have been evacuated from the Russia-occupied city of Oleshky in southern Ukraine. This was announced by US first lady Melania Trump, who was credited with helping to mediate the ceasefire. The mission was coordinated by Ukrainian Human Rights Commissioner Dmytro Lubinets and his Russian counterpart, Yana Lantratova.

Most evacuees chose to be sent to Ukrainian-controlled territory, although some opted for transfer to Russia. Currently, more than 1,000 people remain in Oleshky, according to Kherson's regional administration. They were supplied with food, water and medicine.

Melania Trump hailed the mission as a success, taking to social media to thank Kyiv and Moscow for their "leadership and cooperation." She said the Oleshky ceasefire could go on to inspire a "lasting peace between the two peoples."

Dmytro Lubinez similarly wrote on the Telegram messaging platform that it is "important to maintain this cooperation mechanism," adding that "we are currently exploring options with Yana Lantratova to continue similar humanitarian efforts."

The Kremlin, however, was quick to dampen any such expectations, making clear that no further plans for humanitarian missions exist at this stage.

One day after the ceasefire ended, Ukraine's general staff stated that Russia had "used the humanitarian pause to fortify its positions." It also said Russia had carried out troop rotations, expanded its anti-drone defenses, and brought in ammunition.

British broadcaster Sky News aired drone footage, produced with the support of the Ukrainian army, showing the Russian army transporting heavy equipment, weapons, personnel and food to Oleshky and neighboring villages.

"It was clear to the Ukrainian government and international mediators that Russia could exploit the pause in this way. But it was the right thing to do to allow people to leave the country," Volodymyr Vyatrovych of the Ukrainian European Solidarity opposition party wrote on Facebook.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

He believes Russia would do the exact same thing if Kyiv were to withdraw its troops from the Donetsk region, as Russian President Vladimir Putin is demanding.

"Russia would use this to prepare for its next offensive, to further destroy Ukraine," Vyatrovych wrote. That said, humanitarian missions have their place, says Ihor Chalenko of the Ukrainian Center for Analysis and Strategies. "We shouldn't speak ill of the Oleshky mission, even though it's clear that the Russians have used the ceasefire in the occupied territories to their advantage," Chalenko told DW. That's why he says Kyiv should insist on banning such behavior in future ceasefires.

The Oleshky mission took place just shortly before US voters headed to the polls in the midterm elections. Dmytro Levus of the United Ukraine research center believes the timing was no coincidence. "The Russian side played right into the hands of US President Donald Trump, whose Republican Party was expected to face a crushing electoral defeat," Levus said.

Involving Melania in the mediation was a "subtle public relations move" for Trump, through which he hoped to score points ahead of the election, Levus explained. First Lady Melania Trump has long been an advocate for humanitarian causes.

Chalenko says the Oleshky ceasefire was in Washington's interest because it allowed Donald Trump to cast himself as a "peacemaker."

Moscow also hoped it could improve its image through the humanitarian mission, especially as it caused Oleshky's humanitarian catastrophe in the first place, Chalenko said. Rescuing civilians is intended to serve as proof that "not everything is bad in Russia."

Cooperation between the two human rights ombudspersons had reached "a new level," Chalenko said, adding that the Oleshky case was just one of several noteworthy examples that also included prisoner swaps and foreign representatives gaining access to Ukrainian political prisoners held in Russia.

He does not, however, believe that Russian Human Rights Commissioner Yana Lantratova makes her own decisions. "We are seeing a certain top-down realignment of Russian policy," Chalenko told DW. He also said it was important to further develop this communication channel. The lessons learned from the evacuation of Oleshky could be applied to other towns along the front, Chalenko believes.

Dmytro Levus says the Oleshky mission has proven that, if Moscow were so inclined, a ceasefire could be established along the entire front line. Yet Russia has so far shown no willingness in doing so, partly because Ukraine has not agreed to Putin's terms, Levus explains.

Preparations are underway for a new round of negotiations between Ukraine, the US and Russia on November 10 and 11 in Abu Dhabi. Dmytro Levus believes the Oleshky ceasefire could provide substance to talks on humanitarian issues. He does not, however, think there will be much progress on a political level, even though he does see scope for a ceasefire at sea, for example.

Levus says that the US will be particularly keen to present further humanitarian successes. Ukraine may use the talks to negotiate a new prisoner exchange, though Levus says Russia will likely only budge under pressure.

Ihor Chalenko says one should not expect too much of the upcoming talks. He believes that Russia is looking for a pretext to withdraw from the negotiations, preferring instead to expand its cooperation with the US, without wanting to make any concessions to end the Ukraine war.

Samuel Greene of King's College London is similarly skeptical. "I assume that at some point, the Kremlin will come to realize that there is nothing left to gain on the battlefield and that they will have to reach an agreement."

"That moment hasn't yet come though," Greene told DW, adding that Russia was still trying to buy time by feigning a willingness to engage in dialogue with the US.

Pete Hegseth under fire as Republicans trade blame for midterm debacle

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Iran war hawk Pete Hegseth is facing the fallout from Republicans' midterms losses as high energy prices turned voters toward Democrats. US President Trump continues to support his embattled defense chief — for now.

Republicans in Washington continued to assess the damage on Thursday, after a resounding electoral defeat in the midterm elections two days prior saw Democrats take over Congress by large margins.

Republican Party insiders told US outlets CNN and The Washington Post that Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth is feeling the heat over criticism that the war in Iran, which he supports, cost his party the elections.

Trump on Wednesday acknowledged that the Iran war affected the midterms, saying that voters were frustrated how the 8-month-long conflict has cause gas and diesel prices to skyrocket.

"The Iran thing we had to do, and we continue to do it," Trump told reporters, but he ultimately defended the war effort and denied any plans to fire his defense secretary over it, saying Hegseth was "doing a very good job."

But US Vice President JD Vance has sought to distance himself from the war, questioning whether it was worth the cost.

"The ⁠reason ⁠we lost is the war," Vance said on X. "If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so."

Hegseth has been a loud voice in Washington for the US attacking Iran, and has publicly chastised lawmakers and journalists for raising questions about the conflict's strategic objectives, cost and lack of congressional authorization.

The defense secretary has also been accused of not being forthright about the damage to US bases in the Middle East and the severity of troop casualties. He has also purged dozens of high ranking military officers, while denying promotions to others on dubious grounds.

Hegseth was in hot water even before the Iran war. In October 2025, a Signal group chat between himself and Vice President JD Vance discussing a military strike on Yemen in October 2025 accidentally included a journalist, and led to calls for Hegseth's dismissal.

According to CNN's reporting, multiple Republicans told the network on condition of anonymity that many in their party are now turning against the defense secretary.

Sources also told the Washington Post that the political situation at the Defense Department appears "very dynamic at the moment," with "knives out."

With Trump backing Hegseth for now, The Post reported that those close to the Trump administration have floated the notion that the president might dismiss General Dan Caine, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, or Admiral Brad Cooper, who has overseen the war, instead of the embattled Hegseth.

Pressure is mounting in Capitol Hill, as Hegseth's critics from both sides of the aisle turn their focus on him.

CNN reported that House Republicans are expected to hold an impeachment vote to directly repudiate the defense secretary in a matter of days.

Republican Representative Thomas Massie of Kentucky, who has been a critic of Trump, told CNN he plans to trigger the impeachment, which multiple GOP sources say is expect to pass.

In September, Massie had said he would force a vote in the House on impeaching Hegseth, which led to House Speaker Mike Johnson sending Congress into recess until after the midterms.

On Thursday, Massie posted on X that his impeachment resolution is a "much gentler" version of what's coming when the Democrats take over Congress in January.

"They'll have hearings & trials," he wrote.

Chief Pentagon spokesman Sean Parnell said that Hegseth "will not back down or be deterred by politicians seeking to score partisan points or launch phony investigations where no wrongdoing exists."

"We will continue to drive President Trump's agenda at the Department of War — despite threats of sham witch hunts from Democrats — and Secretary Hegseth is 100% committed to leading the Department until the end of President Trump's term in office," Parnell added, using the administration's name for the Department of Defense.

Fresh off their sweeping wins, Democrats will have the power to launch investigations on Hegseth and even slash funding for the Defense Department and the Iran war.

"I'm going after Pete Hegseth. Not only is he incompetent and corrupt, he's undermining our national security and the safety of our troops," said Representative Jason Crow, a Democratic member of the House Armed Services Committee.

Representative Adam Smith, the top Democrat on the committee, said a top priority would be "to conduct vigorous oversight of the Department of Defense and the Trump Administration," including on "the illegal war against Iran."

A new bill could change how the US builds energy infrastructure

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The midterms are over, and the next big fight could be over permitting reform.

A sweeping bit of legislation designed to cut red tape could speed up the buildout of energy infrastructure in the US.

With the dust barely settled on this week’s momentous midterms, the Senate is set to vote on the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) on November 9. It’s a plan that could finally start an overhaul of the slow, complicated process for getting energy projects approved, as part of something called permitting reform. People on both sides of the political aisle have been pushing for this type of change for some years.

The key question is whether this will cut greenhouse-gas emissions from the US energy sector or push them higher still.

The federal permitting system is designed to protect the environment against industrial pollution from large projects like power plants, transmission lines, and natural-gas pipelines. Today, permitting is governed by a range of laws, including the National Environmental Policy Act and the Clean Water Act. And if there’s one thing you might know about the current state of permitting in the US it’s that it’s slow and incredibly complicated.

So in its 417 pages, BAAJA attempts to tighten things up. The bill has provisions that narrow the sorts of projects that need to apply for federal permits and others that enact deadlines in hopes of speeding up a process that today can take years. (There are also specific provisions about transmission planning, mining, and the rest of the kitchen sink; this thing is dense.)

One thing to know about this bill is that it’s largely technology neutral: It opens the doors for the buildout of projects involving both fossil fuels and renewables. So there’s a weird mishmash of legislators and outside groups supporting it.

The bill’s four sponsors in the Senate are two Republicans and two Democrats. The White House has backed this permitting reform bill, with its Council of Economic Advisors finding that it would reduce review times and have economic benefits like boosting GDP.

Clean energy groups like the American Clean Power Association have largely come out in favor of the bill too, with the group’s analysis finding that it could unlock 350 gigawatts of new clean energy for the grid.

Some environmental groups, however, oppose the bill, or parts of it. An assessment from the Natural Resources Defense Council says the parts about transmission could have substantial benefits for the grid, but the narrowing of environmental reviews could be damaging for public and private lands.

Many in the climate world have long been calling for permitting reform, arguing that renewables stand to benefit more than fossil fuels. The thinking is that solar and wind power are some of the cheapest sources available today, so if we unlock the ability to build more energy, they are likely to win out economically.

A few early analyses have tried to quantify just what BAAJA would mean for emissions in the US. One, from the think tank Rhodium Group, found that in the best-case scenario, the bill would cut economy-wide emissions by 4%, reduce annual energy bills by $78 per household, and add 200 gigawatts of new clean energy by 2040. In the worst case, emissions would stay about the same.

Another report, from the nonprofit research group Resources for the Future, found that the bill could cut power-sector emissions 6% by 2041 and save more than $350 billion on system costs by midcentury.

But there are some concerns that this permitting bill’s real effect would be to open the floodgates for new natural-gas plants. As data centers have put more pressure on the grid, a wave of such plants have been announced.

“You get down to a trade-off between essentially growing the clean electricity industry on the one hand and then, as a compromise, growing the fossil-fuel industry on the other,” says Kevin Kircher, an energy modeler and assistant professor of mechanical engineering at Purdue.

To quantify that trade-off, Kircher also modeled the potential emissions effects of the bill and found that if BAAJA had been in place in 2025, energy sector emissions would have been higher than the actual baseline. Health and mortality risks associated with air pollution would also have increased.

This bill is scheduled for a Senate vote on November 9, well before the next Congress takes control. It’s not clear what the dramatic victories for Democrats might mean for the legislation, which was expected to pass the Senate with largely Republican support if a few Democrats got on board. Democrats could decide to wait for next year, when they’ll have control of both chambers. But any deal would ultimately need to win Trump’s signature, limiting what a future bill might be able to change.

In a world where permitting new projects is easier, we’ll see more clean energy, but we’ll see more natural gas, too. And while prices could get lower, that could push demand for electricity higher, leading to more emissions overall.

Simplifying the permitting system and cutting red tape is a worthy goal. But when it comes to emissions, it still really matters what, exactly, you want to build.

Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged.

These scientists hope that fire-spotting satellites, AI tools and ancient land practices could preserve the world’s vulnerable permafrost—and the vast carbon it stores.

New technology could help cut power losses and reinvent a staple of the grid.

Americans voted against Trump's Iran war. Gulf countries hope that means an end to the conflict

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The US midterm results showed how unpopular Donald Trump's Iran war is. Now, Gulf countries, trapped for months in the middle of the war between the US and Iran, see reason for optimism.

After his party's resounding defeat in the US midterm elections this week, President Donald Trump conceded that the war with Iran had likely played a part.

"I think a part of it was Iran, but, at the same time, people understood the importance of what I've done with Iran," Trump told Fox News, conceding that US voters were unwilling to accept the impact of the war on gas prices.

Observers say the United States and Israel had been holding off on major attacks on Iran until after both had held elections.

At the end of October, the Wall Street Journal reported that the Trump administration was already warning Gulf state governments to brace for a short, intense military campaign after the midterms.

While foreign policy is a US president's purview, analysts have pointed out that a Congress controlled by the Democratic party could ostensibly withhold funding for Trump's military expeditions. However newly-elected Democratic politicians won't take their seats until early next year.

As two US professors of political science wrote for Foreign Affairs magazine this week, past American presidents, like Trump, who become "lame ducks" — that is, a president who loses control of Congress and can't run for another term — have often ramped up international interventions.

"As they think about their legacy, lame ducks often turn to foreign policy, where the power of the presidency allows them to act unfettered by pesky domestic politics," they explained. And one of the places where Trump is most likely to act is Iran, the professors argued.

On the other hand, Trump also told several media outlets that he was in "no rush" to start bombing Iran again.

Opinions on Iran's next moves are also divided.

The Iranians have said they're ready for any attack and rhetoric from senior leaders indicates they may be willing to strike a wider range of targets than previously. That could include regional energy and economic infrastructure and even undersea telecommunications cabling or data centers.

If Iran thinks there is an existential threat to its leadership, then "all bets are off," Ross Harrison, a senior fellow at the Washington-based Middle East Institute, or MEI, and an expert on Iran, said during a virtual briefing hosted by MEI this week. "I think they will target differently in the Gulf states [this time] … so it really depends on how all this unfolds."

But other experts believe Iran was deliberately holding out to pressure Trump before the midterms. Now the election is over, the Iranians may be more willing to negotiate a peace deal.

All this leaves the Gulf states, who have been the main targets of Iranian attacks since the conflict began, in a difficult position.

"We find ourselves drawn within a crisis that we worked diplomatically very hard to try to avert," Anwar Gargash, a foreign policy adviser to United Arab Emirates leadership, said at a security conference in Bahrain last weekend, the Manama Dialogue hosted by the International Institute for Strategic Studies, or IISS. "We are clearly seeking a political off-ramp."

Qatar has been trying to negotiate between the US and Iran but has also been conducting emergency drills at its airport. The Emiratis have been doing similar drills. And Saudi Arabia is looking at a war on two fronts: The oil-rich state is already under attack from the Houthi militia in neighboring Yemen. Even Egypt has reacted, reportedly strengthening air defenses around the Suez Canal.

Still, no matter what happens next, the US-Israel-Iran war has already made Gulf states evaluate defense partnerships with the US. Rather than making them safe, US bases have made them a target.

"There is widespread disappointment across the region with the US over the folly of the war, the erratic nature of Washington's military and diplomatic conduct, and its decision to prioritize Israel's defense over that of its Gulf partners," Hasan Alhasan, a senior fellow at the IISS, told news agency Bloomberg this week. "If the US liberation of Kuwait in 1991 was the moment that established America's role in the region, the 2026 war may be remembered as the moment that undid it."

Gulf states are widening their outreach with, for example, the UAE getting closer to Israel and the Saudis entering the Mecca defense pact with Pakistan and Turkey.

"The Gulf states have already moved toward strategic autonomy and self-reliance," Andreas Krieg, a senior lecturer at the School of Security Studies at King's College London, wrote for online outlet Middle East Eye this week. "A lame-duck Trump will speed that process."

That's even though a Democrat-led Congress might make the US a slightly more reliable partner again.

Krieg argues that Gulf states should come together to pressure the US not to attack Iran and then negotiate a non-aggression pact with Iran — without US involvement.

Whether that kind of unity is possible in the Gulf is dubious, Ryan Crocker, a former US ambassador to the region and distinguished fellow with MEI, replied to a question from DW during the MEI's online briefing.

"I'm afraid that meaningful Gulf unity is not coming to a theater near us anytime soon," Crocker said. The Qataris remain untrusting because of how other Gulf states acted against them in 2017. And, he added, "there are well known differences between Saudi Arabia and the UAE we see in Sudan [and] in Yemen, and, of course, the profound differences … on relations with Israel. So I would not expect any meaningful moves toward [Gulf] security cooperation."

What's more likely in the long term is Gulf states eventually coming to some kind of understanding with Iran.

"The Gulf states understand that they probably can't buy their security only from Washington, and that they're going to have to buy it, to some degree, by some kind of a relationship with Tehran," the MEI's Harrison added. "It's inevitable. Geography reigns."

What that sort of non-aggression pact looks like remains to be seen, he added. That's why one of the big concerns for Gulf leaders now, is the conflict escalating again to a point where the path back to some sort of diplomacy is lost, Harrison concluded.
