Gavin Newsom commutes sentences of all death row inmates in California

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Gavin Newsom, a Democrat and a rumored presidential candidate, moved to commute the death sentences of 548 inmates in California for one of his last acts as governor of the state.

California Governor Gavin Newsom said Thursday that he will change all death sentences in the state to life in prison without the possibility of parole.

"I take this action because I firmly believe that state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California's values, undermines the foundations of our legal system, and diminishes each of us," Newsom said in a letter to Californians.

There are 548 people on death row in California.

The outgoing governor added in the letter that he has long opposed the death penalty and issued a moratorium on executions in 2019.

The California state constitution requires the California Supreme Court's approval to grant clemency to anyone convicted of a felony more than once, which means the court would have to approve 382 of those commutations, or roughly 70%, according to numbers provided by Newsom's office.

Newsom's announcement also comes after the failed execution of Christa Pike renewed scrutiny of the death penalty.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Newsom, who is rumored to be considering a run for president in 2028, said the decision was among the most difficult choices of his career because it will surface painful memories for crime victims' families.

"As Governor, I alone bear responsibility for this decision and its impact, just as I alone would bear responsibility for each execution," Newsom wrote.

He acknowledged that Californians had voted to keep the death penalty. In 2016, 53% of people rejected a ballot measure that would have abolished the death penalty in the state.

"But the constitution also gave every governor clemency authority, and that power exists for moments when law as applied cannot be squared with justice," Newsom said. "For me, this is that moment."

The American Civil Liberties Union (ACLU), who has said the implementation of the death penalty reflects racial bias, applauded the announcement.

"These commutations reflect what justice demands: an end to a racist, error-prone, and inhumane punishment that has burdened California for far too long," said Anthony Romero, the executive director of ACLU.

In 2025, 17 countries carried out a total of at least 2,707 executions, according to Amnesty International.

The new sentence against Benko, 49, comes just ‌three weeks ⁠after ⁠he was released to house arrest to serve out a previous conviction.

French protests see numbers surge as unions join strikes over high cost of living

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The head of France's CGT labour union, Sophie Binet (C), said the wave of protests and strikes had reached a new level

More than 600,000 protesters have taken part in rallies and marches across France, according to France's interior ministry, with five unions joining a day of action demanding more money for wages and public services.

The left-wing CGT union put the number of people taking part in Thursday's mobilisation at 1.57 million and hailed the figure as "a level unmatched" since 2023, citing big protests in Paris, Marseille, Bordeaux and Lille.

As darkness fell in Paris, Prime Minister SÃ©bastien Lecornu said he would convene a "social conference" with unions and employers next Monday.

The wave of protests began in September over poor conditions and resources at lycÃ©es (high schools), before spreading to universities, workers and a revived "yellow-vest" movement against high prices.

The unions had vowed to take the co-ordinated move against the government "to the next level", and CGT chief Sophie Binet had said their message was that "the only thing that will bring this mobilisation to a halt is responding to our demands".

Interior Minister Laurent NuÃ±ez had earlier deployed 70,000 police, vowing "an extremely firm response" to any potential outbreaks of violence.

Some unrest was reported in several cities.

A car was set alight near the Gallieni bridge over the RhÃ´ne river in Lyon, as hundreds of activists from the radical left-wing "black bloc" clashed with police.

Clashes were also reported in the north-western city of Rennes, where the local prefect said a "hostile group of masked individuals" had gathered in front of the main union march. Tear gas was used to clear a square in the south-western city of Bordeaux and a teenager was hurt in a stabbing involving a student at a high-school blockade in the Seine-Saint-Denis suburb of northern Paris.

But the trouble was not on the scale seen during the last big day of protests, on Saturday 17 October, when more than 2,000 stores were looted and historic buildings set on fire.

The provisional numbers reported on Thursday were higher than on 17 October, considered the biggest day of rallies until now when the interior ministry said 550,000 people had turned out and the unions counted 800,000.

Earlier on Thursday the leader of the CFDT union, Marylise LÃ©on, said she would be happy if the protests matched that earlier level.

A car was set alight during a protest in the centre of Lyon

A coalition of unions across the public and private sector had urged workers, pensioners and young people to join Thursday's day of strikes to intensify pressure on the government. In particular they are seeking a rise in the minimum wage, or Smic, which is currently â¬12.31 (Â£10) an hour.

Sophie Binet told reporters at the start of a march in central Paris that protesters had "just reached a new level in their mobilisation" and were demanding immediate answers.

An estimated 9.89% of civil servants were taking part in the strikes, according to government figures.

Disruption was reported in schools, hospitals and public transport, and the Eiffel Tower and some exhibition rooms at the Louvre museum in Paris had to close.

Workers were also asked to halt production at oil refineries and France was expected to import more electricity than it produced on Thursday because of disruption at nuclear plants.

Disruption was already reported at schools on Thursday morning

The head of the CFDT complained that it was not just the minimum wage that needed to increase. "We are asking for a net increase of â¬100 a month," Marylise LÃ©on explained.

The government has sought to appease public anger over rising prices, bringing down the cost of fuel by releasing 10 million barrels of diesel from strategic reserves. Ministers have also promised school students to reform the unpopular Parcoursup university admissions platform and eventually replace it by 2028.

The prime minister said early on Thursday evening that Monday's "social conference" with unions and employers would seek to "provide answers to questions surrounding purchasing power and wages".

However, the government by its own admission has little room to manoeuvre, as it looks to make tens of billions of euros in savings, in a bid to cut the budget deficit to 5% of GDP.

The education ministry said that 16% of teachers had gone on strike on Thursday, and the unions said disruption had affected more than one in four lycÃ©es, which cater for 15-18 year-olds, as well as the vast majority of universities.

"Now it's not only about [investing in] public services in education and higher education, it's about all of the public services in France," the vice-president of the Unef student union, Alice De Brito, told the BBC.

Meanwhile, a revived gilets jaunes (yellow-vest) movement that staged a revolt against the high cost of living several years ago has called for further blockades and protests on Friday and Saturday too.

Thursday's march was eventually allowed in central Paris, although not along the route proposed by the unions between Place de la Bastille and Place de la Concorde, which would have taken protesters near the National Assembly, the ÃlysÃ©e Palace, and the prime minister's residence at HÃ´tel Matignon.

Instead, the march followed another central route from Place de la RÃ©publique to Place de la Nation.

California governor commutes sentences for state's 548 death row inmates

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

California Governor Gavin Newsom's term in office comes to an end in January

California Governor Gavin Newsom has commuted the death sentences of all 548 people on death row in the state, changing them instead to life in prison.

"We can now let these offenders die in obscurity while we forever remember and honour the victims," he said in a letter.

The decision to spare death row inmates is largely symbolic in a state that has not executed an inmate since 2006, though experts say it could save millions of dollars in court costs each year. Only 13 inmates have been executed since voters reinstated the death penalty in 1978.

The decision comes as Newsom, the Democratic leader of the most populous US state, has been discussed as a possible 2028 contender for president.

Newsom, who is term limited, is winding down his final months in office. Fellow Democrat Xavier Becerra is projected to take over after the midterm elections on Tuesday.

Newsom explained in his letter that he is exercising his constitutional clemency authority to commute the sentences to life without the possibility of parole.

"After nearly 30 years in public office, I have come to understand the limits of what government can justly accomplish," Newsom said. "I take this action because I firmly believe that state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California's values, undermines the foundations of our legal system and diminishes each of us".

California has historically had the largest population of death row inmates. Newsom had previously ordered a halt to the death penalty in the state in 2019.

Americans' views on death penalty after failed execution in Tennessee

Describing his decision as a difficult but "righteous action", Newsom said that although many Californians feel that those who commit brutal crimes deserve to die, he has concluded that "the state cannot morally kill them".

He said that by every measure, California's system for administering the death penalty "is a failure", and that it has not and cannot be fairly applied, resulting in the conviction of innocent people.

Life without the possibility of parole is the most severe punishment he can lawfully impose and that the state can ethically enforce, he said.

He acknowledged that his clemency action will anger and hurt some Californians and said that it will "always weigh heavily on me".

"As governor, I alone bear responsibility for this decision and its impact, just as I alone would bear responsibility for each execution," he wrote.

"For this reason, I am compelled to follow my conscience and, as authorised by our Constitution, take this action for the welfare of the State of California."

Because individual states have the power to prosecute crimes and order executions, much of the power lies with state governors.

Bernard E Harcourt, a law and civil liberties professor at Columbia University, told the BBC that it is not unprecedented for a potential presidential candidate to come out as an abolitionist in the run up to elections - despite the overall popularity of the death penalty in the US.

He noted that FranÃ§ois Mitterrand did precisely that in his presidential campaign in France in 1981, taking "the higher ethical ground and was rewarded by the public for that".

"I detect a very similar ethical positioning in Governor Newsom's statements about the commutations. And here too, it may well be something that the voters respect," he said.

San Quentin State Prison's used to house California's death row adjustment centre

Newsom's decision comes after the high-profile botched execution of a woman in Tennessee in late September and while executions have been on the rise in the US. There are three executions scheduled for Thursday in Indiana, Florida and Alabama.

At least four of seven judges on the California Supreme Court will have to sign off on the commutations, CalMatters reported.

It is unclear how Newsom's decision will affect condemned inmates like Keith Doolin, 53, who was sentenced to death in 1996 for murdering two sex workers, Inez Espinoza and Peggy Tucker, and shooting four others. He maintains his innocence.

Doolin lived on death row at San Quentin for decades, until he was moved to another prison in 2025 when the governor announced the state would reform the prison for all but those on death row.

Doolin's mother, Donna Larsen, told the BBC that the news of Newsom's commutations - which includes Doolin - brought mixed emotions.

"At least the execution chamber will not be reconstructed, and no more killing by the people of the state of California," she said.

Anthony D Romero, the executive director of the American Civil Liberties Union, applauded Newsom's action.

"These commutations reflect what justice demands: an end to a racist, error-prone, and inhumane punishment that has burdened California for far too long," Romero said.

"By taking this step, Gov. Newsom has affirmed that the death penalty has no place in California and he showed real courage by confronting racialised injustice head-on."

Nathan Hochman, the top prosecutor in Los Angeles County, said that while Newsom has the power to commute sentences, only state legislature or voters can eliminate the death penalty.

"Californians have been asked on two separate ballot initiatives to abolish the death penalty and both times they have chosen to keep it for the most serious crimes," Hochman said in a statement.

How widely used is the death penalty in the US?

On the federal level, President Donald Trump, a long-time supporter of the death penalty, has described capital punishment as "an essential tool for deterring and punishing those who would commit the most heinous crimes and acts of lethal violence against American citizens".

In 2020, during his first term, he ended a nearly two-decade moratorium on the federal death penalty, by ordering 13 executions over a six-month period.

While the moratorium returned under President Biden, on the first day of Trump's second term in 2025, he signed an executive order that re-instated the death penalty.

No federal executions have been carried out since he signed it, however. In the US, both the federal government and local states authorities can order executions.

The death penalty is legal in 27 states, but has been abolished in 23 states and Washington DC, according to the Death Penalty Information Center, a national non-profit.

California, which primarily uses lethal injection for executions, used to house male death row prisoners in San Quentin State Prison north of San Francisco, and female inmates in the Central California Women's Facility in the Central Valley.

But a 2016 ballot measure allowed for their transfer to other facilities that provide a necessary level of security, according to the California Department of Corrections and Rehabilitation.

Why the death penalty is on the rise in US

US inmate Christa Pike says she woke up 'confused and in pain' after failed execution

French protests: 'It's only when public, private sector act together that you can make changes'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

“You can be as repressive as you want. The grievances are still here.” With this stark observation, Ariane Bogain, lecturer in French and politics at Northumbria University, captures the central dilemma confronting the French government: how can political authority be restored when the social and economic grievances driving public anger remain unresolved? Bogain and French politics editor Marc Perelman join Delano D'Souza to examine a protest movement that has evolved beyond its initial demands over purchasing power, fuel prices and education into a broader challenge to the French government. Perelman identifies a striking political paradox: Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc Mélenchon, despite occupying opposing ideological positions, are “objective allies” in their shared electoral interest in weakening the political centre.

Hoax bomb threats target European airlines and airports for second time in a week

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Aer Lingus plane at Evreux military air base in France after being diverted following a bomb threat

At least 21 aircraft and several airports across Europe have been targeted by bomb threats in the second such incident this week, with the UK calling it a "malicious hoax".

Flights to Austria, Belgium, Bulgaria, the Czech Republic, France, Italy, Poland and Romania were affected. An Aer Lingus plane from Dublin to Rome was diverted to an air base in France and Italian fighter jets escorted Finnair's Helsinki-Rome flight.

EasyJet said its flights from Newcastle and Glasgow were met by police in Prague as a precaution. London Heathrow and Birmingham airports received threats that were assessed as not credible.

No explosives have been found during searches, and it is not clear if the incidents, some claimed by a little known group, are related.

Who could be behind hoax bomb threats to European air travel?

The airport authorities in Italy's capital Rome said the hoax threat against two inbound flights received on Thursday had come by email.

The warnings were signed on behalf of a group or individual calling themselves "Fighters of the Male State", claiming to be targeting a "feminist regime" and wanting to "make our mark on history".

They threatened to "turn the skies red" with explosions.

These details were first reported by Italian media and then confirmed to the BBC by Aeroporti di Roma, which manages both of the city airports.

"Fighters of the Male State" likely refers to the Russian group "Muzhskoi Legion" ( "Male Legion") - an ultra-right group with such extreme views it was banned by a Moscow court in 2021. Yet it retained tens of thousands of online followers as it cheered on the full-scale Russian invasion of Ukraine in the following year.

But that email, even if genuine, is unlikely to provide the leads that police and airport authorities need to pin down who is generating these hoaxes and why.

UK transport secretary says flights hit by 'hoax threats'

UK Transport Secretary Heidi Alexander described Thursday's incidents as "malicious hoax threats."

She said the government was taking "these threats seriously", stressing that there were "established processes for dealing with hoax and malicious threats".

A UK government spokesperson later said the threats reported "in the early hours" of the day had been "resolved", without giving any further details.

Heathrow later said in a statement it had received a threat earlier in the day which was "deemed not credible", adding there was "no risk to passengers, colleagues or the aircraft".

"There are robust UK aviation security standards in place to assess and safely manage these types of situations," the statement said.

Meanwhile, a Birmingham Airport spokesperson told the BBC it had received a threat, after which "processes were immediately initiated in conjunction with emergency services and partners".

The following flights were targeted by threats on Thursday:

Aer Lingus said its flight from Dublin to Rome diverted to Normandy, landing safely. The company added that it was working to "re-accommodate customers to their final destination as soon as possible".

Two Finnair flights from Helsinki landed at their destinations as normal, the airline said. One was escorted into Rome's Fiumicino airport by Italian Air Force fighter jets, Aeroporti di Roma told the BBC, while the other landed in Vienna, Austria

Wizz Air said two flights which departed Milan were affected - one was heading to Warsaw, before being diverting to Vienna; the other landed safely in Bucharest, with teams inspecting the aircraft

Polish airline LOT said its flight from Warsaw to Brussels landed as planned, and "no suspicious items were found". Another LOT flight from Warsaw to Paris was stopped before take-off and the plane and baggage underwent a security screening, with no hazardous items found

Three Ryanair flights landed in Bulgaria, according to Bulgarian media - one from Copenhagen and another from Madrid landed in the capital Sofia, while a third coming from London bound for Sofia landed in Plovdiv. The flights were escorted by Bulgarian air force fighter jets

No explosives were found on four flights which landed in Prague, Czech police said. EasyJet later said its flights from Newcastle and Glasgow were among those affected. "Passengers on both flights disembarked normally," the company said

Aegean Airlines said it "was informed of a bomb threat" concerning two of its flights from Thessaloniki in Greece to Milan, and also from Athens to Vienna. It said threat was "unfounded", and both flights departed from the respective airports"

A Ryanair flight from Porto in Portugal landed safely in Milan as planned, with bomb disposal experts checking them on arrival, Italy's Ansa news agency reported

Spain's airport authority said it had received bomb threats affecting three flights at Madrid airport, but any threat was ruled out following inspections. Iberia later said two of its flights - Milan-Madrid and Rome-Madrid - were affected. It was not clear whether the third plane was the above reported Ryanair Madrid to Sofia flight

Air Serbia said its flight from Belgrade to Bucharest "landed safely at its destination airport", and after checks, a reported bomb threat was "assessed as unfounded"

Emily Noble, who was aboard the diverted Aer Lingus plane from Dublin to Rome, said she was about an hour into her flight to attend a friend's wedding in the Italian capital when passengers were told about a "technical" problem.

Later, as the Aer Lingus plane began circling for an unplanned landing in France, the pilot said there was a "security" issue, the 26-year-old from Dublin told BBC Verify.

On board it was "an odd experience," Noble said. "It was worrying because we didn't know what was going on, but we were all trying to be as calm as we could."

Noble says she could hear fighter jets during the landing and saw one touch down behind the aircraft.

After landing at the Ãvreux-Fauville air base in Normandy, passengers learned the flight had been the subject of a bomb threat.

Emily Noble, a passenger on a diverted Aer Lingus flight, told the BBC she was now waiting for transfer to Rome at an air base in France

Noble said they remained on board for two-and-a-half hours while police and military personnel carried out checks, including searches with sniffer dogs.

The passengers were later told they would be taken to an airport in Paris and another plane would fly them on to Rome.

Philip Baum, professor of aviation security at the University of Coventry, said the industry "faces multiple bomb threats every single day" but he had been "surprised" by this week's incidents.

A flight is diverted "only when the threat is credible and the authorities believe there is a potential of explosive device on board", he said.

He said the concern was that "if you issue enough hoax threats, then complacency could set in and a real threat could be ignored - or they could be a diversion".

Marco Chan, an aviation expert at Kingston University, told the BBC the threats were "most likely a hoax, but the system is right to treat it as real".

He said they were being viewed by some experts as "possibly part of a pattern of cheap, deniable disruption" to air travel.

Hoaxes can also come from copycats, he said, but "three days of incidents is too small a sample to separate coordination from contagion".

Two days ago, seven European flights, including two from the UK, were searched after bomb threats were made. No explosives were found on any of the planes after searches.

On Monday, a Wizz Air flight travelling from London Luton Airport to Sofia was escorted by a Hungarian fighter jet to Budapest after a mid-air bomb threat.

Major tech stocks plunge as US midterms add to fears of AI slowdown

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Hundreds of billions of dollars in market value has been wiped from major tech firms in a stock market rout after the US midterm elections sparked concerns of a slowdown in AI development.

Following Democratic wins after election campaigns run on slowing AI, shares in firms across the tech and artificial intelligence sectors plummeted.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq composite stock index closed down by more than 5.5% to lose an estimated $2.5tn in market capitalisation, according to Dow Jones data, while other key US stock indexes also saw big losses in their worst days since 2025.

The sell-off comes on top of broader concerns about whether the huge amounts being spent on AI will pay off.

Democrats in races across the US campaigned to slow the onslaught of data centres and AI, and there were dozens of measures on ballots around the country aimed at restricting the development of the hubs crucial for powering the technology.

Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa campaigned heavily against data centres, pledging a moratorium on new construction in the sector.

Shares in companies heavily exposed to AI data centres, including crucial chipmakers and semiconductor manufacturers, fell sharply on Thursday, highlighting investor concerns that had already been growing.

Nvidia shares slid more than 9%, knocking more than $400bn off the firm's market value.

Other chipmakers also suffered, with AMD and Micron both dropping by more than 17%. Intel shares also dropped, down 10.3%, while memory card maker SanDisk plunged more than 22%.

The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index, which tracks the performance of 30 of the largest companies involved in semiconductor production, fell by more than 12%.

Shares in Elon Musk's companies SpaceX and Tesla also plunged, falling 8% and nearly 11% respectively and shaving more than $300bn off the firms' market value on Thursday.

Musk had spent nearly $450m backing Republican campaigns, including at least $45m in support of Ken Paxton for the Texas senate seat, but Texas was turned blue in the Democratic tsunami on Tuesday night.

In total, the so-called magnificent seven tech companies, including Apple, Nvidia and Tesla, have shed a trillion dollars in value in a single trading day.

The stock falls also hit other major US stock indexes. The S&P 500 index fell by 4.4% by the end of trading, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average erased gains made on Wednesday to close down more than 3.4%.

The Nasdaq Composite fall was so large it is now in what market watchers call correction territory, which is where an index or company value falls at least 10% below its recent high.

Live: What can Democrats do with control of Congress? We answer your US midterms questions

Food, fuel and taxes â can the Democrats actually make life cheaper?

Boebert, billionaires' tax and other midterms races still to watch

Susannah Streeter, chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, said the steep sell-off on the Nasdaq and the S&P 500 was sending shockwaves into other markets.

"Fresh panic over the AI trade has hit Wall Street hard," she said.

"Fears that the vast sums poured into AI infrastructure may not deliver the returns investors have been banking on are now colliding with the prospect of a sharp slowdown in data centre construction."

Some tech leaders have said they were caught off guard by the backlash against data centres in the midterms.

OpenAI president and co-founder Greg Brockman said he believed everyone had been surprised by the reaction to data centres, and more work needed to be done to ensure communities were on board.

"A lot of our shift over the course of this year has really been to realise that from San Francisco trying to build infrastructure in Texas, Georgia, and other places, you can't just tweet and say: 'This is going to be good.' You need to be in those communities," Brockman said at the WSJ Tech conference in Napa, California on Wednesday evening.

Meta's president Dina Powell McCormick said that the industry needed to put more effort into selling AI as a good idea.

"I think we need to double and triple down on telling the story," she told the WSJ conference.

Alongside the immediate impacts of the US mid-term elections, some investors have become concerned that AI shares could be in a bubble that is due a correction.

Europa League: Sunderland lose at home to Zagreb while Celtic enjoy first win

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Evanilson helps Bournemouth to 3-1 win at Sparta Prague

Ferencvaros prevail 2-0 at Milan to keep 100% record

Sunderland’s unbeaten run in the Europa League came to an end as they were beaten 2-0 by Dinamo Zagreb at the Stadium of Light.

Dinamo went ahead through Luka Stojkovic’s fierce strike and the midfielder turned provider for Dion Drena Beljo to double their advantage before the break.

Sunderland had opportunities to get back into it as Wilson Isidor’s header was chalked off for offside two minutes after the restart and Kevin Danso hit the crossbar before Brian Brobbey went close.

However, Régis Le Bris’ side ultimately fell to their first defeat in four Europa League matches to continue a worrying run of form, with the club bottom of the Premier League.

Substitutes Callum Osmand and Camilo Durán sparked Celtic into life as the Scottish champions got off the mark in the Europa League in a 3-2 victory over Besiktas.

Martin O’Neill’s side were heading for a seventh defeat in 11 matches and bottom place when Junior Olaitan netted on the rebound after Sam Johnstone had saved his 54th-minute penalty.

But Osmand drilled home the equaliser with his first touch in the 62nd minute, seconds after coming off the bench in a triple change, and the 20-year-old set up Durán to put the hosts in front.

Besiktas substitute Orkun Kokcu equalised from long range but Celtic went back in front within 60 seconds when Durán stabbed home an 87th-minute winner from six yards.

The win hands Celtic a fighting chance of progressing especially with Portuguese second-tier side Torreense as their next opponents.

Bournemouth extended their winning run to seven matches as Evanilson’s wonderful late goal helped earn a 3-1 win away to 10-man Sparta Prague in the Europa League.

Marco Rose’s side have enjoyed a dream start to the club’s debut European season and they will be confident of securing direct passage to the last 16 after making it four victories from four, but they were made to work hard for the points in the Czech capital.

They went behind in the first half to a piece of individual brilliance by Gambian forward Ebrima Singhateh, minutes after Lewis Cook had spurned the chance to give them the lead when his penalty struck a post. But Tobias Guddal let his team down badly when he picked up a second yellow card within two minutes of his side taking the lead.

Bournemouth had not conceded a goal on their six-game winning streak and they responded to the setback of falling behind by punishing Guddal’s error, levelling in the 57th minute when Tyler Adams smashed a loose ball into the bottom corner.

From then on there looked like being only one winner. Evanilson’s 81st-minute effort, finished low across the goalkeeper, put them in front before Rayan added gloss with a third at the death.

Fabian Hürzeler saluted a professional performance from Brighton after they beat Getafe 1-0 in the Conference League.

Malick Yalcouyé scored his fourth goal of the season to break the deadlock in a hard-fought encounter in Madrid.

The Seagulls are now the only team not to have conceded a goal in the competition so far and their seven points from three matches leaves them fifth in the table.

“It’s what we expected,” said Hürzeler. “These games in the Conference League are different. You have to beat the low block. We didn’t play our best game, to be fair. But in the end we kept a clean sheet – the fifth clean sheet in a row in this tournament – and then we scored the goal and I think we deserved to win this game.”

The Swiss side Lugano are top of the Conference League, with maximum points after a 3-0 win over Kauno Zalgiris at the AIL Arena.

Panathinaikos beat CSKA Sofia 2-1 to also make it three straight wins, Twente beat 10-man Pafos 3-1 and Monaco saw off Nordsjælland 2-0 to extend their own 100% start.

Ajax ran out 6-1 winners away to 10-man Midtjylland, while a depleted Hearts side lost 3-1 away to Thun in Switzerland.

Milan lost 2-0 at home to Ferencvaros as the Hungarians kept pace with Bournemouth, maintaining their 100% record in Europe.

Haiti international Lenny Joseph struck twice in the second half at San Siro to earn Ferencvaros a deserved win over the seven-time European champions despite having Bamidele Yusuf sent off.

Milan are languishing outside the top 24 with just three points at the halfway stage of the league phase. “I won’t give up on this competition. As long as we’re not mathematically out of it, we’ll keep fighting in this competition, just as we do in the others,” Milan’s coach, Ruben Amorim, said.

Inside Ye’s Yeezy Runway Show in Los Angeles After His Antisemitism Scandals

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Once I clawed my way through the crowd of leather pilot jackets and passed the overzealous security guards who checked my ticket thrice, I walked through a borehole into the new Yeezy flagship store in Los Angeles’s Koreatown, where Ye stood off in the corner talking to some professional cool kids. Hundreds of people gathered on Wednesday night inside the white, brightly lit, cavernous space connected by tube-like passages for his label’s first runway show in four years. Rappers Big Sean, Kid Cudi, Rich the Kid, and A$AP Nast showed up—so did influencers Quenlin Blackwell and Fai Khadra. The streamer N3on, Ye’s daughter North, and his wife Bianca Censori also attended. And there were hundreds of unticketed fans waiting outside, hoping to get in.

“I’ve been waiting for a Yeezy show for a minute,” Kid Cudi told a camera crew on his way inside. “It’s just beautiful to see my big bro back out there doing his thing.” The musician’s team said he was “on hiatus” when Vanity Fair reached out for comment afterward. A woman standing next to me online started loudly complaining as people tried to cut her. “I just wanna see Ye,” she moaned.

And there were some other attendees who seemed more interested in making their own content. “I’m trying to network,” N3on told his Kick audience on a livestream from the show. “Everyone’s here bro…I’m looking for some collabs.”

The show started with the sound of the first note (E6) of the piano part at the beginning of Ye’s song Runaway. Models marched around a giant mound in the middle of the room in leather trench coats, militaristic jackets, shorts cut like briefs, and monochrome combat uniforms in shades of bone, charcoal, black, and red. Some of the models wore chunky combat boots that rose up the calf; others balanced on thin stilettos, including a pair of knee-high cream boots that looked like they belonged to a space-age dominatrix. It was utilitarian and a bit fetishy. The pacing of the runway walks was relentless, as if Ye was hitting you over the head with this concept until you thought it was cool. After the show, Ye walked out to a standing ovation, wearing an all-black leather outfit, black military boots, and a huge gold tiger-shaped belt buckle.

There is something fascinating about the fact that Ye can still draw adoring crowds like this one in Koreatown, and sell out football stadiums while other artists find themselves on black lists for doing way less (Javier Bardem, for example, claims he’s lost roles and brand endorsement deals because of his opposition to the war in Gaza). Ye’s last major runway show was in October 2022, during Paris Fashion Week, when he sent models down the runway wearing shirts emblazoned with the words “White Lives Matter.” The stunt led to a public confrontation with then Vogue fashion editor Gabriella Karefa-Johnson, whom Ye proceeded to attack online after she criticized the collection (Vogue, like Vanity Fair, is owned by Condé Nast). The whole thing, plus his breakup with Adidas (a brand that was started by two former members of the Nazi party) over a series of antisemitic comments, amounted to a huge fall from grace for someone who once enjoyed the patronage of Anna Wintour and helped reshape the next generation of streetwear.

One reason for Ye’s continued commercial viability is, of course, his artistic prowess. There are certain artists who become too big to fail, not because they’re too wealthy or too powerful, but because their old work has become so deeply embedded in popular culture that people can’t let it go, while their new work continues to push boundaries. That might be the case for Ye—even if this latest show wasn’t particularly groundbreaking or original. If I’m being generous, the new line was giving Boots, the Netflix military dramedy set in the ’90s about a gay marine. But, given what we know about Ye, parts of the show could’ve also been interpreted as skinhead-coded. And it certainly didn’t feel like I experienced a display of genius—a term we need to stop using to describe Ye. He probably doesn’t need us to put him on that pedestal (his ego is big enough), and I remain skeptical that a truly great artist can be capable of spewing so much hate. Art is about empathy.

I suppose most of the people cheering at Wednesday’s show had learned to compartmentalize. They had found a way to separate the designer who completely changed the sneaker game and ushered in a new era in streetwear from the guy who declared his love for Hitler, threatened to go “DEFCON 3” on Jewish people, and released a song called “Heil Hitler.” Or perhaps they really believed the apology Ye published as an advertisement in The Wall Street Journal earlier this year, in which he attributed his antisemitic behavior in part to bipolar disorder and a brain injury. Or maybe the antisemitism never bothered them in the first place.

I was asked to cover this show because I’m based in LA—I’m typically not a style correspondent. But still, I was plenty curious to see what Ye was up to. On the drive over, I thought about the ethics of covering his first big commercial venture since 2022. Maybe I would be giving him exposure he didn’t deserve. Or, maybe my presence amounted to some kind of tacit endorsement. But I dismissed that thought because I knew plenty of Jews who went to Ye’s concert at NRG Stadium in Houston last week, and I didn’t equate their attendance with bigotry. But it raises a lot of interesting questions about the politics of art and fandom. Does going to see the Michael Jackson movie mean you don’t believe the sexual abuse allegations against him? Do I have to forgive Ye’s antisemitism if I want to keep The College Dropout and My Beautiful Dark Twisted Fantasy in my listening rotation? Maybe it’s kind of similar to how carnivores need to be okay with the murder of animals if they’re going to eat meat. I guess I’d have to be willing to forgive Ye in some sense if I plan to keep singing “couldn’t afford a car, so she called her daughter Alexis,” out loud on the StairMaster at Equinox.

Whatever one thinks of Ye personally, his influence on fashion and music is undeniable. The muted colors, exaggerated proportions and futuristic sneakers that helped define Yeezy have spread through the industry. His fingerprints are all over contemporary fashion, including the celebrity brands that followed in his wake. (Cough cough: Skims and SKYLRK).

Wednesday’s show, titled Season 01, was billed as a reset for Yeezy. So, it makes sense that the show took place in something of a primordial cave. But it’s going to be hard for him to ditch some of the baggage he’s accumulated over the last few years. Standing outside the show was a man wearing a navy suit, white shirt, red tie, and Trump ’28 hat—a loud reminder that Ye hobnobbed with white supremacists like Nick Fuentes and ran for president with the far-right conspiratorial political commentator Milo Yiannopoulos as his campaign manager.

Ye perfectly summed up my feelings about him in one of his own songs. “I miss the old Kanye, straight from the Go Kanye.”

Anthropic’s November IPO Plans Face Investor Push for Better Terms

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Reuters reported on November 5, 2026 that potential investors in Anthropic’s initial public offering are questioning whether the AI company can begin its planned roadshow in the week of November 9, 2026 and complete a November listing, citing four people familiar with investor discussions.

Investors are pressing for a more attractive valuation before committing to the offering, with some treating the recently discussed $1.5 trillion valuation as the upper end of the acceptable range. Anthropic declined to comment on the doubts.

The roadshow had initially been expected to start that week, with pricing the following week. The people familiar with the discussions said Anthropic might need to offer a bigger discount to secure commitments. If AI-related assets remain under pressure, the company could wait for a market more likely to absorb an offering that some expect could reach $100 billion.

The valuation pressure marks a shift from earlier in 2026, when investors expected an October IPO seeking a valuation as high as $2 trillion. The debate now involves both price and timing: even if Anthropic and its investors agree on valuation, as Wall Street expects, market conditions could still determine whether the company proceeds by year-end.

Anthropic’s November 2, 2026 amended registration statement remains preliminary, with the number of shares and initial price range blank. It reported $17.0 billion in revenue for the three months ended September 30, 2026; the document does not set the offering’s final size or price.

Two of the people familiar with the investor discussions said investors are awaiting Anthropic’s October revenue figure, which was left blank in that update, to assess whether growth has strengthened after a recent slowdown. The missing figure therefore bears directly on the pricing discussions.

Investors’ push for a bigger discount also reflects Anthropic’s need to raise capital, its record of quarterly losses and its large commitments to partners in the AI buildout. Those pressures help explain why they are seeking more favorable terms despite the revenue disclosed in the filing.

The investor discussions follow what Reuters characterized as the most severe technology-stock selloff in a year, alongside geopolitical uncertainty affecting new offerings. More than 50 S&P 1500 technology stocks fell by at least 10% during the November 5 session, and Vanda Research said retail selling was the strongest since the COVID-19 pandemic.

Keith Lerner, chief investment officer at Truist Advisory Services, said technology had underperformed the S&P 500 by 10.7% over the preceding month, the most extreme one-month underperformance since May 2002. He described the selling as painful but still within normal market behavior.

Support for the offering has not disappeared. Many investors remain bullish on AI infrastructure, and some analysts expect strong retail demand for Anthropic. Rick Meckler, a partner at Cherry Lane Investments, said he expects interest in major private technology companies to persist when they go public, although he also expects some pullback from private-market valuations.

Reuters assessed that a delay could intensify concern about the AI ecosystem and squeeze valuations across a stock market heavily weighted toward large technology companies if Anthropic were seen as struggling to raise funds.

The immediate hurdle is the gap between Anthropic’s capital needs and investors’ demands for a discount, with the October revenue figure and market conditions central to the negotiation. Whether Anthropic can close that gap without postponing the roadshow is the open question hanging over its planned November listing.

Can Portugal Retire Cristiano Ronaldo’s No.7 Shirt

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Many wondered if this news would ever come, but yes, Cristiano Ronaldo has finally retired from international football. Now 41, the veteran forward has played his final game for Portugal.

The Al-Nassr star bows out with 146 goals in 234 caps for his country, having also won Euro 2016. In his departing message, he said: "To the Portuguese people, the day has come to return the number 7 jersey of our national team. I never felt it was exclusively mine. But for 23 years I had the privilege and responsibility of wearing it, defending it, and taking it with me to stadiums all over the world. Today the moment has come to hand it over to the future. It wasn't an easy decision, because there are decisions that aren't made only with the head. They are also made with the heart."

For club and country, the number seven shirt has become synonymous with Ronaldo. As such, despite his claims that it's 'time to return the jersey,' some are wondering if Portugal will instead retire the number as an acknowledgement of his special career.

However, it might not be quite so simple as just making one decision.

The 41-year-old has retired from international football, havign scored a record 146 goals in 234 Portugal caps.

A little-known FIFA rule stops international shirt numbers from being retired. Ronaldo actually started out wearing No.16 when he made his debut in 2003, but was handed the No.7 shirt in 2008. He wore it as he lifted Euro 2016 and two Nations League titles, while also becoming the all-time men’s international top goalscorer.

Had he achieved that level of success at a club team, maybe he would have seen the shirt number retired. Johan Cruyff (14 at Ajax), Paolo Maldini (3 at AC Milan), and Franco Baresi (6 also at Milan) are all among the legendary footballers to have had their shirt numbers retired, while Liverpool paid respect to Diogo Jota by retiring his No.20 jersey after his tragic passing in 2025.

Ronaldo, though, will have to pass on the number seven shirt to another Portuguese star. This is because national teams are required to designate consecutive numbers for every player in the squad for FIFA and FIFA confederation tournaments. At the 2026 World Cup, for example, sides were made up of 26 players, meaning squad numbers had to go from 1 to 26. As such, it would be a breach of rules to skip past the number seven on a tournament-going squad list.

Perhaps this isn't the worst rule in the world. After all, Argentina once tried to retire the No.10 shirt in honour of World Cup-winning great Diego Maradona. They had a request denied by FIFA ahead of the 2002 World Cup, but the number was fittingly later won by Lionel Messi, who would win the 2022 World Cup and match his predecessor's legacy.

Despite also having many past greats, Portugal have never requested to retire a number before. Ballon d'Or winner Luis Figo used to wear No.7, while the likes of Nani and Ricardo Quaresma even donned it while Ronaldo was absent.

It's worth noting that when Ronaldo quit the Portugal camp early in September, Rafael Leao took on the vacant No.7 shirt for the two remaining Nations League fixtures. It remains to be seen if he will wear that number in the upcoming break later this month.

Cloudflare shares surge as revenue growth accelerates to 39% and outlook rises

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Shares in Cloudflare Inc. jumped more than 14% in late trading today after the connectivity cloud company smashed third-quarter expectations and lifted its full-year outlook well above what Wall Street had been modeling. The gain more than wiped out an 8% fall in the stock during the regular session.

For the quarter ended on Sept. 30, Cloudflare reported adjusted earnings per share of 38 cents, up from 27 cents in the same quarter of 2025, on revenue of $782.5 million, up 39% year-over-year. Analysts were expecting earnings of 34 cents per share on revenue of $736.8 million.

Revenue growth hit its fastest pace in nearly four years as the annualized run rate crossed $3 billion. Current remaining performance obligations rose 37% from a year earlier. The adjusted operating margin also improved, to 17.5% from 15.3%, after shrinking year-over-year in each of the two prior quarters.

Because stock-based compensation ran to $174.1 million, Cloudflare still lost $34.1 million on an unadjusted basis, compared with a $1.3 million loss a year earlier. Another $11 million went to restructuring from the roughly 1,100 job cuts announced in May. Free cash flow slipped to 10% of revenue from 13% a year ago.

On the product side, short-lived blocking rules that write themselves during a live attack are the core of Adaptive Intelligence, which Cloudflare added to its Bot Management product in late August. AI agents got tools of their own earlier in the month through Cloudflare Wallets and cloudflare.pay, which give an agent an identity handle and will let it pay for services from a stablecoin balance. Federal agencies can now put more sensitive workloads on Cloudflare for Government, which reached FedRAMP High status in August.

Co-founder and Chief Executive Matthew Prince called the period “Cloudflare’s best quarter ever” in the earnings release. Cloudflare has built the development platform for the AI-powered internet, and organizations of every size keep turning to it for protection as digital threats escalate, he said. Prince added that the business has “found an even higher gear for this next phase of growth.”

Cloudflare also named Christian Smith president of revenue today, with responsibility for worldwide sales and revenue operations. He replaces Mark Anderson, who had already said he would retire from the full-time role at year-end. Smith previously ran sales at Splunk Inc. as chief revenue officer.

For the fourth quarter, Cloudflare expects adjusted earnings of 43 cents per share on revenue of $845 million to $846 million. Analysts had forecast 38 cents per share on revenue of $798.2 million.

Cloudflare now expects 2026 revenue of $2.963 billion to $2.964 billion, about $97 million above the midpoint of the range it gave in August. Analysts had been looking for $2.87 billion in revenue and $1.26 in adjusted earnings per share for the year, and the company is now guiding to $1.35.

Treasury bought back $6B in bonds, but dealers say bigger isn’t enough

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Treasury's latest $6 billion buyback shows why Wall Street dealers want the government to rethink how it buys bonds, not just how much it buys.

The US Treasury bought back $6 billion of older government bonds on Nov. 4, hitting the full limit of its latest operation. On paper, that looks like a success for a program designed to make the Treasury market easier to trade.

But there's a catch. One bond accounted for $4.9 billion, or 82% of everything Treasury agreed to buy. Add another $950 million from a second bond, and just two securities made up almost 98% of the entire purchase. Of the 40 bonds eligible for the operation, only seven were bought.

This is the kind of concentration Wall Street dealers have been worried about for months. In feedback published by Treasury on Nov. 4, dealers said the larger buybacks were helping market liquidity.

However, they also explained why simply increasing the program size might not be enough to achieve that.

Their feedback came before Wednesday's operation, so they weren't responding to this particular result. We now know the Treasury can buy billions of dollars of bonds and still leave dealers holding the securities they hoped to sell. The amount purchased is important, but so is how those purchases are spread around.

The Treasury market is enormous, but not every government bond trades equally easily. The newest issues attract the most attention and typically have the deepest markets. Older bonds, known as off-the-run Treasuries, can be harder to sell quickly without accepting a lower price.

That's where dealers come in. They buy bonds from customers, keep them on their books, and look for someone else willing to take them.

But holding those bonds costs money, sometimes a lot of money. That's why dealers often have to borrow to finance their positions, exposing them to price changes while they wait for another buyer. The longer they hold a bond, the more those costs and risks compound.

Treasury's liquidity-support buybacks give them another potential buyer: the government itself.

In September, Treasury began larger buybacks of longer-dated bonds, at least doubling the previous $2 billion maximum for those operations. The idea was to provide a more reliable outlet for older securities.

According to the Nov. 4 dealer feedback, most dealers reported at least modest improvements. They said the purchases had made it easier to find an exit for older bonds, reduced the risk of sudden price distortions, and helped keep pricing relationships between similar securities more stable.

While that's meaningful progress, the Treasury got mixed answers when it asked whether the changes had improved dealers' ability to handle customer trades.

A lack of unity among dealers is what makes these buybacks so controversial. We now have dealers that welcome a large government buyer but aren't necessarily becoming more willing to buy bonds from customers.

If the Treasury keeps concentrating its purchases in a handful of issues, dealers holding other, older issues still have to find other buyers or keep financing the position. In other words, the buyback program can work without working equally well across the entire market.

Dealers wanted better, earlier communication about operations, smaller purchases more frequently, and limits that would help distribute Treasury buying across a wider range of individual securities.

More frequent operations would give dealers more opportunities to sell, as they'd get another chance sooner.

Limits on how much of one security the Treasury can buy could also prevent a single bond from absorbing most of the available capacity. Each bond has its own identifying number, called a CUSIP, so dealers suggested using per-CUSIP limits to spread purchases more broadly.

Some dealers specifically warned about the risk of missing a concentrated buyback and being left to manage the interest-rate exposure from bonds that remained on their books.

This is exactly what we saw on Wednesday. According to the official data, Treasury received $39.6 billion in offers for an operation capped at $6 billion. That means dealers and other eligible participants offered more than six times what Treasury ultimately accepted.

And despite that demand, one bond maturing in November 2041 absorbed more than 80% of the purchases.

Nov. 4, 2026 operation, accepted face value in USD. Labels identify coupon and maturity. All seven purchased securities are shown; 40 were eligible. Eligibility does not show which unpurchased issues were offered. Face value is not cash expenditure. The largest share is calculated as $4.924 billion / $6 billion. Settlement: Nov. 5.

Source: U.S. Treasury: Nov. 4 buyback result, pages 1–2

It's worth being careful about what those numbers tell us. Treasury doesn't publish the full breakdown of rejected offers, so we don't know whether every one of the 33 eligible bonds that received no purchases was actually offered. We also can't assume Treasury made a bad decision by concentrating its buying.

Start with the stories that matter. Get the context behind the headlines and a clear view of what to watch next.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

This is because buybacks are extremely competitive operations. Treasury evaluates the prices offered and the relative value of the securities, so if one bond presents particularly attractive offers, buying more of it may make sense for taxpayers.

The problem is that getting the best available prices and helping the widest range of sellers aren't necessarily the same thing.

Nov. 4, 2026 operation, USD face value. Treasury accepted $6 billion against $39.636 billion offered, filling the $6 billion maximum. These overlapping measures are compared, not added. The offer ratio is 39.636 / 6 = 6.606, rounded to 6.61. Offered face value does not count distinct sellers or unique inventory. Filling the maximum is not a measured liquidity improvement.

Source: U.S. Treasury: Nov. 4 buyback result, operation totals

Treasury also has existing limits designed to preserve the amount of individual securities circulating in the market. Dealers are asking for changes that could distribute purchases more evenly within those constraints.

They discussed other possibilities, including alternative pricing methods and transactions that would exchange one security for another. But those would be more complicated to introduce, and Treasury said they would require further work.

For now, the most immediate requests are simpler: communicate earlier, buy more often, and avoid having so much of the operation go to just one security.

For investors watching Treasury buybacks as a potential source of liquidity for Bitcoin, a $6 billion buyback doesn't automatically mean $6 billion in fresh liquidity is headed toward risk assets.

First, the $6 billion is the face value of the debt Treasury agreed to retire. Because those bonds were trading below face value, the cash purchase amount was approximately $4 billion before accrued interest.

Second, Treasury continues issuing new debt to finance the government even as it buys back older securities.

So the program primarily changes which bonds are outstanding and how easily dealers can manage them. It doesn't automatically increase the amount of money available to buy Bitcoin.

However, a connection may still exist through financial conditions. An Oct. 30 Federal Reserve study, using data through June 2025, found that Treasury buybacks reduced dealers' bond inventories and eased some risk constraints for trading desks.

But the researchers found no statistically significant reduction in dealers' repo activity.

Repo is essentially short-term borrowing backed by securities. It's one way dealers and their customers finance bond positions. That's important because taking bonds off dealers' books doesn't necessarily eliminate the financing demands tied to the broader market.

For Bitcoin to benefit, easier Treasury trading would need to translate into better financing conditions or greater investor capacity to take risk. Wednesday's purchase result alone can't tell us whether that happened.

The next question is whether Treasury changes the program in ways that make those improvements more consistent.

In its latest quarterly plan, Treasury said it anticipates up to $70 billion in liquidity-support buybacks from November through January, alongside a separate $25 billion for cash management purposes. It also confirmed that it's evaluating changes to purchase limits, scheduling, frequency, eligibility, and execution.

That gives dealers a chance to see whether their feedback leads to something concrete.

The real market test won't be whether Treasury manages to spend its full buyback allowance. It'll be whether more bonds become easier to trade, dealers become more willing to handle customer orders, and financing pressures actually ease.

A $6 billion buyback might look impressive on paper, but for a dealer still holding a bond nobody wants to buy, the announcement's size only goes so far.

'We were quite lazy' - Fabian Hurzeler gives brutal Brighton verdict after narrow win in Getafe

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Malick Yalcouye fired Premier League high-flyers Brighton to a 1-0 win at Getafe but head coach Fabian Hurzeler was not pleased with the performance.

The impressive Yalcouye scored his fourth goal of the season to break the deadlock in a hard-fought encounter.

The Seagulls are now the only team not to concede a goal in the Conference League so far and their seven points from three matches leaves them fifth in the table.

Brighton’s win in Madrid concludes a gruelling run of five consecutive away matches where they travelled to Jablonec, Liverpool, Manchester City (twice) and Getafe.

Hurzeler said his team deserved to win but also said they were lazy at times.

“That's what we expected,” said Hurzeler to TNT Sports after the match. “These games in the Conference League are different. You have to beat the low block, you have to understand what the opponents are looking for in the game and therefore you have to always be patient in these games and I think we did it today.

“We didn't play our best game, to be fair, we had a few moments where we were quite lazy with the easy things, we didn't do the simple things on the highest level, but in the end, you said it, we kept a clean sheet.

Hurzeler added: “The third clean sheet in a row in this tournament and then we scored in the right moment the goal and I think we deserve to win this game.”

Boss Fabian Hurzeler made five changes to the side which beat Manchester City at the weekend with Yalcouye joining Jaouen Hadjam, Michael Svobada, Jack Hinshelwood and Georginio Rutter in the starting line-up.

With the stadium undergoing renovation only 570 Brighton fans could attend, squeezed into a small corner of the north stand behind one of the goals.

They had a good view of Hinshelwood taking the first shot of the match, a 25-yard drive tipped wide by Getafe keeper David Soria.

Rutter then rolled defender Sebastian Boselli but skewed his shot across goal and wide.

They threatened again when Hadjam got in behind, only to lose his footing when he tried to cut back inside, with Rutter unable to convert the loose ball.

Brighton were almost caught out from a Getafe corner on the half-hour mark with Bart Verbruggen forced into a sharp reaction save from Ivan Azon’s snap-shot.

The Seagulls should have scored when Rutter’s through-ball sent Hadjam racing clean through. Hadjam got to the ball before Soria but his finish clipped the keeper and came back off a post, with Yalcouye’s attempt from the rebound blocked on the line.

Azon threatened the Brighton goal again in first-half stoppage time but was once again denied by Verbruggen.

After the break some good build-up play from Rutter and Hadjam set up substitute Luka Vuskovic, but his shot was always curling away from the target.

The breakthrough arrived in the 64th minute when Pascal Gross lifted a diagonal ball towards the far post.

Overlapping full-back Costinha had acres of space on the right of the penalty area and cushioned his header back across goal.

Yalcouye’s timing was impeccable as he met the ball with a thumping header which flew past Soria and in off the crossbar.

Ibrahim Osman could have doubled the lead seconds after coming on but he could not make contact with Diego Gomez’s low cross.

As Chargers' offense struggles, Mike McDaniel will move from the sideline to the booth

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Chargers offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel will get a different perspective on his struggling offense on Sunday against the Texans.

McDaniel said today that he will move upstairs to the booth to call plays after previously working from the sideline.

“One of the things that I want to try is call plays from the box to see adjustments for myself and communicate with the position coaches appropriately what things we can focus on between series,” McDaniel said. “I won’t be in the same spot.”

Although the Chargers’ offense has been among the worst in the NFL this season and head coach Jim Harbaugh has faced questions about whether he should fire his offensive coordinator, McDaniel insisted that he’s not concerned about the criticism.

“My problem solving doesn’t come from insecurity,” McDaniel said. “I’m not trying to prove or justify anything.”

The Chargers rank dead last in the NFL in scoring, having scored only 99 points this season, and they have an NFL-high 17 turnovers. McDaniel said he thinks it’s worth trying something different while delegating some of what he had been doing on the sideline to the assistant coaches.

“Seems like a good action to take based on how things have gone this year, and realistically something that I’ve only brought up because I’ve observed, I’m very confident in the rest of the offensive coaching staff and how they’re able to set the tone and communicate information on the sidelines. So I feel a little more comfortable to try that,” McDaniel said.

If that doesn’t work, Harbaugh’s next step might be to move McDaniel from the booth to the unemployment line.

T-Mobile owner on Elon Musk launching a wireless carrier: &#8216;Welcome to the party&#8217;

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Starlink Mobile could cost SpaceX at least $200 billion, Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges says.

Starlink Mobile could cost SpaceX at least $200 billion, Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges says.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The head of T-Mobile’s parent company is casting doubt on SpaceX’s plans to make Starlink Mobile a “major” carrier in the US. During an earnings call on Thursday, Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges says he takes the Elon Musk-owned company “super serious,” but that it will cost at least $200 billion to pull off:

He [Musk] has now bought 76 megahertz of spectrum. By the way, we have 400. So if you now say he can buy the spectrum prospectively, do your math. These are kind of triple-digit billions which are needed to gain the spectrum position. If he has the money, go for it. Then he has to build a network. I can tell you, we have 85,000 towers. No, he can rent towers, no question. But this is another $20 to $30 billion which are required.

Put antennas on it. You can buy antennas… and you can get [them] maybe even in a cheaper way, or you construct it in a new way, but you need money for that. So therefore, my assessment is that SpaceX at least — if they want to play in that industry — needs at least minimum $200 billion. But even beyond that, to play in an industry which we have, let’s say, professionalized over the last 20 years — okay, welcome to the party.

Last month, SpaceX laid out its plans for Starlink Mobile to compete against T-Mobile, AT&T, and Verizon by using a combination of direct-to-cell satellites and a terrestrial network. One recent report suggests that SpaceX could tap into 80,000 cell towers throughout the US, while another indicates that Musk was in talks to acquire Boost Mobile. This week, Qualcomm announced that SpaceX will use its cellular infrastructure technology to support Starlink Mobile’s network buildout.

At the same time, Höttges is aware of the potential threat Starlink Mobile could pose to the mobile industry by creating a “new” type of service.

“He [Musk] has chipsets, he has data centers, he has AI, he has satellites… He will go as well for maybe integrating social media or at least agent models into his service. So he is creating a new way of communication services,” Höttges says. “That is why I’m saying I take that super serious because I was witnessing what happened to the car industry. They were ignoring it for too long, and we have to face the situation. But it’s not a free lunch. And you see that with Tesla as well in the car industry.”

New West Records Names Brad Turcotte as General Manager

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The label's artist roster includes American Aquarium and Amos Lee.

New West Records has named Brad Turcotte as general manager. The label’s artist roster includes Billy Joe Shaver, Amos Lee, American Aquarium, 49 Winchester and All Them Witches.

Most recently, Turcotte has served in roles including artist manager at Vector Management, senior vp at Australia-based live entertainment promoter TEG Live and partner at marketing agency 615 Leverage + Strategy.

Turcotte has also previously held leadership roles at major labels including UMG Nashville (now MCA) and Big Machine Label Group. Along the way, he’s worked with artists including Taylor Swift, Chris Stapleton, Keith Urban, Luke Bryan and Carrie Underwood. The move to New West Records marks a return to Turcotte’s indie label roots, which began with his founding of Houston-based Compadre Records in 2001.

“This year has brought a few unexpected detours for me professionally, but I truly feel like the road has ended at the best home possible,” Turcotte said in a statement. “I can’t thank John Allen and George Fontaine Sr. enough for trusting me with this role. Many moons ago, when I opened Compadre Records in Houston, one of our main competitors was another young independent label just down the road in Austin called New West Records. We were both trying to build something meaningful, champion great artists and make our mark on the music business. To now have the opportunity to help lead New West feels like an incredible full-circle moment.”

Turcotte also serves on the board of directors for the nonprofit music education program Music Will, which aids music education in the U.S. public school system.

Taiwan’s Content Agency Chief Sue Wang Puts IP Commercialization at Center of Strategy

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sue Wang, chair of the Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA), says Taiwan’s content industry should make IP development and commercialization its top priority.

“The challenge is not simply whether we can create good content, but whether companies have the capabilities and business models to turn creative ideas into sustainable revenue, build scalable businesses, and reach audiences across different markets,” Wang says as the 2026 Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF), which runs Nov. 6-9 in Taipei, gets underway. “Without a viable business model that has been tested in the market, simply introducing more funding or investment programs will not necessarily create a sustainable growth cycle.” Related Stories 'Love Language' Review: Chloë Grace Moretz Sells Words of Affirmation in an Old-School Romcom With Gen-Z Trappings Despite Hudson Williams, Canadian Witch Mystery 'Yaga' Is No 'Heated Rivalry': TV Review

Wang says that without IP with demonstrated market potential, investment risks stay high, and even overseas distribution may not turn into sustained sales. TAICCA wants to help businesses strengthen their pricing, licensing, branding and audience-development capabilities first, then bring in growth capital and international distribution.

As of Oct. 30, TAICCA had invested about NT$560 million ($17.1 million) in 24 international co-financing and co-production projects across feature films, series and animation, with partners in 15 countries and territories. One example is Taiwan-Japan film “Arrested Memory,” selected for Venice Open – Out of Competition at the 83rd Venice Film Festival and the Midnight Madness section of the 51st Toronto Film Festival.

TCCF’s Market will bring together 107 exhibitors from seven countries and territories. Exhibitors in IP categories such as publishing, comics and characters have increased 13% from last year.

The first Spotlight Screenings will present “April” on Nov. 7 and Taiwan-Singapore series “See You at 11pm” on Nov. 8. Wang says the goal is to raise the international profile of Taiwanese actors and give buyers a chance to meet the actors and creative teams behind the titles.

This year’s Forum, themed “Reshape,” will feature 12 talks and panels with more than 20 experts on topics including international co-production, streaming consolidation, IP, short-form content and AI. TCCF’s Pitching, Market and Forum sections connect projects with development support, investors, production resources and buyers. Selected Pitching teams may also apply for early-stage support through TAICCA’s Cultural Content Development Program.

Wang says TAICCA wants to deepen ties with France in Cultural Tech, building on more than a decade of cooperation. The Taiwan-France Cultural Award will mark its 30th anniversary in 2027. With South Korea, the agency hopes to expand collaboration beyond screen content into independent music, live venues, festivals and the performing arts.

The Taiwan-Korea Entertainment and Cultural Content Fund and the Far EasTone Creative Entertainment Fund’s Enjoy Entertainment Limited Partnership have approved investments in 12 projects, mobilizing about NT$600 million ($18.3 million) in private capital, Wang says. Projects include the Kaohsiung Park Music Festival, developed with CJ ENM’s Seoul Park Music Festival; the 2026 ID. Music Festival, in collaboration with Spotify; and the concert IP “Her Voice: The Fearless Female Voice.” On the film side, the funds have backed road movie “Lonely Together” and “The Rope Curse 4: Kuntilanak,” the latest installment in Taiwan’s “The Rope Curse” horror franchise.

TAICCA sees AI mainly as a supporting tool for technical work such as animation, visual effects and post-production. Wang says the agency is cautious about generative AI in core creative work, including scriptwriting and music composition, citing uncertain public acceptance and the amount of human correction AI-generated material can require.

“There is no established playbook or ready-made solution for navigating the changes currently taking place in the industry,” Wang says. “We don’t want to see a situation where people end up working as assistants to AI.”

Over the next three years, Wang hopes to see several medium-sized or larger production companies emerge, each producing more than 10 titles a year. She also wants international co-production to become a regular practice and more private and technology-sector investment to flow into Taiwan’s content industry.

America's Real 1% Has Nothing to Do With Silicon Valley or Wall Street

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The wealthiest people in America aren't who you think they are. Together, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by a factor of 10 — and almost no one has heard of them.

In this episode of Motley Fool Hidden Gems Investing, Motley Fool contributor Rachel Warren talks with Eric Zwick, professor of economics at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business and co-author of The Everywhere Millionaire, about the decade of research that rewrote what he thought he knew about wealth, inequality, and the American economy.They discuss:
