This podcast was recorded on Oct. 25, 2026.

Eric Zwick: When you collectively add them up, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by an order of magnitude, so more than 10 times the collective net worth of the Forbes 400, even though individually, they're not as rich as Elon.

Rachel Warren: That was Eric Zwick, Professor of Economics at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business. On a group of Americans almost no one has ever heard of who collectively hold more wealth than the entire Forbes 400. I'm Motley Fool analyst Rachel Warren. Eric spent a decade working through economic data that most researchers never get access to, and what he found completely rewrites the story of wealth in this country. We get into who actually makes up the real 1%, what this hidden economy of Main Street millionaires means for investors watching the public markets, and the statistic that made Eric rethink everything he thought he knew about inequality. We hope you enjoy.

Welcome back to Motley Fool Conversations. I'm Motley Fool analyst Rachel Warren. Today, I'm delighted to welcome Eric Zwick, Professor of Economics and Finance at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business to the show. Eric is also the co-author of an incredibly eye-opening new book, The Everywhere Millionaire. Who is really rich in America and how they got there? Eric has spent a decade digging through economic data to shatter the myth that transformational wealth belongs only to Silicon Valley tech founders and Wall Street executives. Instead, this research has uncovered America's hidden 1%, the regional business owners, pass-through entity founders, and Main Street entrepreneurs, building generational fortunes completely away from the spotlight. Eric, it's wonderful to have you on the show. Welcome.

Eric Zwick: Thank you so much for having me. Great to be here.

Rachel Warren: One of the things that struck me is the core thesis of The Everywhere Millionaire challenges the popular narrative that massive wealth creation requires building a software giant or managing a hedge fund, and there are many other examples. When you look at the data, what does the typical member of America's real 1% actually look like?

Eric Zwick: Yes, so we're told that the path to prosperity is through Silicon Valley or Wall Street, you have to be a tech CEO or a VC-backed start-up founder or a Wall Street trader in order to get really rich in America. What we found through a decade of research, working with unprecedented administrative data that then in the book, we turned into stories by going out and finding these Everywhere Millionaires lurking in plain sight. Wealth is much more pervasive. It's much more abundant across the country. There are people running unglamorous businesses in towns and suburbs and Tier 2, Tier 3 cities all across the country, who are incredibly wealthy. Our average millionaires have an average net worth of around $25 million. They have minimum net worth of about $5 million. They are people who own a private business, and we estimate there are about three million of them.

That's a lot of people. When you collectively add them up, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by an order of magnitude. More than 10 times the collective net worth of the Forbes 400, even though individually, they're not as rich as Elon. They're just collectively this huge group of people that we really didn't know that much about, I think, for various reasons that we can talk about. Concretely, who are they? We do something we joke because we both have little kids, on and I, the ABC board book. The ABCs of getting rich. A is for auto dealers, B is for beer distributors, C is for contractors. D is for dentists and it could go on. But it's like that type of mid market business owner across a whole bunch of industries that are not super glamorous. These are the Everywhere Millionaires whose stories we try and tell in this book.

Rachel Warren: I think in the book as well, you call this group the stealthy, wealthy. I wonder why has this segment of the population managed to build this wealth, but very much away from the spotlight and even the radar of Wall Street?

Eric Zwick: We call them stealthy, wealthy, and some are maybe nefarious a little bit in their behavior, but many, they're just flying under the radar, but just not with anything untoward about their behavior. We call them the stealthy wealthy just cause people aren't looking for them in the right places. Looking away from the coasts. A lot of our attention is drawn to the coasts, where we tend to look in the media at public company activity. A lot of retail investors listen to a podcast. Public companies are specific subset of the American economy, and they disclose their information. You know what they are. They tend to be consumer-oriented businesses, not always. They tend to be in more capital-intensive sectors, so they have to raise capital to go public. This is not a representative pick of the American economy, and it's not the ballpark in terms of where people work, where the profits are.

More than half of the total profits in business across the country are in what we call pass-through businesses. These are non-public businesses. There's a ton of activity. Most businesses in the country are these pass-through businesses, non-public businesses, and they’re all over the place. I think the Forbes 400 is another example of when people think about who's really rich, they jump to the top of the Forbes list. Those are tech founders, typically public companies owners, and also people who are seeking attention in some cases by buying media companies. Our stealthy, wealthy are not actively trying to hide, although some are. One car dealer is notoriously referred to as Batman because everybody knows that he exists, but nobody's seen him. I think they just are running their businesses. They're not in these media markets, and they're doing their thing and enjoying it in a lot of cases, without a lot of attention at all.

Rachel Warren: One of the things that struck me I saw there was a comment on your book from the Nobel Prize-winning economist Angus Deaton, and he was saying that in his comment, there's currently five million households in America with over $5 million each running entirely below the radar. I wonder, what does that tell us about just the scaling capacity, but also the overall health of the U.S. consumer economy?

Eric Zwick: I think there's this fisheye lens view of the U.S. economy that is prevailing in the media narrative where it zooms in on Elon or zooms in on a few billionaires. Then it's like everybody is being left behind. I think the message from these stories is that there's a lot more opportunity for people to do incredibly well. We're not talking about people who just have a million bucks in a pension. We're talking about people who don't really have to think too hard about how they spend their money at this point. They're close to post economic, if not post economic, to use a term of art, where they basically, I want this. I'm going to buy it. There are millions of people like that across the country, and many of them weren't born with a lot of opportunities.

We figure that about a third we try and estimate this in various ways of our Everywhere Millionaires inherited businesses or come through that shortcut into prosperity as business owners, but that means the two thirds or maybe 70% either founded their business or bought one and grew it later in their careers. That's a lot of self made stories in the data. We tell some of these stories individually, **** Portillo from Chicago, who started out growing up in the Cabrini Green Housing Project and started a hot dog stand. He didn't actually know how to make hot dogs at the time. He didn't know how to steam a bun. Had an $1,100 investment initially. Built this thing incrementally with no outside investors, very little debt, just plowing his profits back into the business. It was the largest privately owned restaurant company by the time he eventually sold it because he didn't really want to pass it on to his kids. He was business, and so he was I'm going to sell it in his 80s, although he was annoyed to do it after selling it, he's told us he was bored. But he sold it for over a billion dollars, and we found him in yacht registration data.

He has a very large yacht, a 100-foot yacht called Top Dog. There are stories like this. He's an outlier within that class because he's more like a billionaire that people haven't heard of. But there are just tons of stories that look like him in the data, and I think it's quite inspiring, and it says that there's more opportunity available than I think the prevailing narrative. I think it's an optimistic take on the economy that you don't hear as often as maybe we should.

Eric Zwick: Silicon Valley tech apps, influencer economy. These are terms that I think are the flashy Instagram reel images that we see about rich people. We don't see the trash bag liner manufacturer who supplies to all the McDonald's franchises. Rachel Warren: From your research into these Main Street businesses, I wonder what are some takeaways or a framework that maybe you can draw into evaluating public companies? If you're a retail investor listening to this conversation, what are maybe some lessons that can be learned from these private businesses that can be drawn into the health of the public markets?

Eric Zwick: That's a good question. I like that question. One is probably to think about competition a little bit more broadly. There's scope for competition from the private market in certain product lines. Thinking about when you're picking investments, it's not just about competition from other publics. It's competition from products that are being offered just by these Everywhere Millionaires and their businesses, and which ones are robust to that competition? Which ones have an opportunity to disrupt the sleepier Everywhere Millionaires in certain industries.

I think we've seen legal services, companies that are trying to enter and disrupt, say, these law practices across the country because there's tons of profit there. If you're looking for opportunities or growth, I think disrupting some of these businesses where they're not so focused on staying ahead or adopting new technology. I think it's quite interesting. We've seen a little bit of that with the software as a service. Is this going to be disrupted by AI or is it going to be complimentary with AI? I think we could think about some of these more tactile industries, fast casual dining or auto parts, or mid market manufacturing, making frozen quiches, like this stuff in big box stores.

Is AI going to disrupt all aspects of that business or is AI going to help with customer relations management is going to help with sales? What does that mean for some of the other stocks I'm looking at or some of the opportunities I'm looking at? I think it's useful to have a reminder that the economy isn't just zeros and ones and chips and code, that there's this much richer in the world three dimensional economy out there, and the stories might be a little different than the Magnificent 7. I think, as a public market investor, that's what we think about all the investors are flooding into those seven bets. What are some, maybe interesting contrarian bets? There's some thoughts I have on that question.

Rachel Warren: One of the other things that I was thinking about often, when we look at the public markets, we see some companies expend massive amounts of capital on dilutive acquisitions or even poorly time share buybacks. I wonder how do the stealthy, wealthy, as you call them in the book? How do they tend to think about capital allocation? Because I also think that there's often parallels that we can draw when we're looking at quality businesses in the public markets from what we see in terms of the actions taking place in the private markets.

Eric Zwick: I see some of these acquisitions or throwing capital back at investors through buybacks, it's the consequence of having run out of good ideas. In some cases the dilutive acquisition is, well, we see the writing on the wall with our main business line, which is trending down. In the absence of this acquisition, it's going to look even worse. It could still make sense to do it, even if it's not a great, it's not going to lead to salvation for the company. Stealthy, wealthy folks have the benefit of being able to grow without reinventing themselves in a lot of cases, as I mentioned, geographically or adding a few products like the chemical importer exporter that we profile can just find a few more customers can tell you hard to do, but because their market shares are so much smaller, it's an easier problem to solve to try and grow that business, at least conceptually. Sales is not easy if nobody's heard of you, so you really got to get in the door and go to the customers and talk to them and work that network for sure.

But they have a bit of an easier job growing, especially if they're in a growing industry, so then they can just ride those tailwinds. Big public company that's very established market leader. Dilutive acquisition might be the only game in town, or returning money to shareholders might be the only game in town. I think maybe the stealthy, wealthy version of that is, well, reaching the end of my life cycle going to retire. I don't know who's going to take over the business, so maybe I'll just sell this thing to private equity and ride off into the sunset on my yacht. That's what some of those stories look like. It's returning the money to shareholders, as opposed to burning the thing down to maintain control.

Eric Zwick: I came at this thinking about the stories on especially like top inequality was maybe one of the early things that we thought about. Piketty’s Capital had just come out, that book that was all about. We're back in the Gilded Age. It's a new Gilded Age. A few billionaire industrialists that are taking over the whole economy. Which in Picketty's book, the top 1% is this nameless, faceless group of people. You don't really get a lot of humans. You're, Oh, this is not the typical one percenter or even 0.1 percenter is not like the money bags monopolist, Gilded Age person. It's like a private business owner with a mid market business. There are tons of these people. The top 1%. When we looked at narrow industries, the industry generating the most profits for their owners was doctors' offices. I was, Oh, doctors' offices, that doesn't feel like a capital intensive business at all.

That's really just they are running practices, they've structured as businesses, so they can pay themself a bit lower wages, but higher profits get better tax treatment, but also scale the business up and sell some of the services and so on, that doctors do. But there's just a ton of doctors, and healthcare is 18% of the economy, and a big chunk of it is not hospitals or pharmaceuticals. It's just services being provided by these people. I was, Okay, this is a totally different view on inequality and who's really rich than I entered and you just trying to answer that question. That blew my mind.

The more we talk to people or tell them about what we're learning in the data, not just economists or academics, but just like friends, family, people that we encounter, conferences, and so on, people that I play golf with. They just know all these people. They know these stories. They recognize the stories pretty quickly from their own lives, from their own backgrounds, from where they grew up, from people they know, their regional banker, from who they're lending to, the magnet manufacturer, this kind of thing. I think you realize they're all around us and we just haven't really been looking for them in the right way. It was very eye-opening to me, and I think a lot of people who have read the book, which was written not just to be collected, but also to be enjoyed and read, will have that eye-opening experience, I believe.

Rachel Warren: One final question for you as well. Just as investors who are hearing all of this and drawing on the lessons that you've showed us today, I also wonder, what are some key elements, metrics we can be looking at tracking the health of the economy, tracking the health markets. If I'm an investor listening, trying to see how can I be tracking the health of the economy and the markets over the next 12-18 months? What would you be looking at?

Eric Zwick: That's good. Thinking about these Everywhere Millionaires, some of the more aggregate, tangible retail sales, consumption data. These businesses are often selling, say, the fast casual dining. You're looking at those kinds of metrics. That's not just quarterly earnings, but really what's going on with consumption? Are people able to spend money or people pulling back because prices have gone up? I think that's an important thing going forward on the economy and that's where through a lot of these types of businesses, they'll grow if customers keep coming in the door and they'll stop growing. They won't close, but they'll stop growing if traffic slows down. Then if you're in the supply chain, there are a lot of cost shocks. We're looking at some of the PPI numbers to think about where are inputs, especially tight.

What are some of the second and third order effects of, say, energy prices going up? Well, fertilizer goes into food, so food costs, or you have all the distribution logistics and trucking and so on, that uses fuel. Well, that means like everything that moves around that needs to go from warehouse to consumer, warehouse to store. That's going to be more expensive. You track some of that stuff. A lot of them Main Street millionaires are running the businesses along that supply chain or in those input providing. You can see in some of the aggregate data that comes out of the statistical agencies, you can see the health of the economy that way. I think those are some of the problems that we face today. I'm looking at some of those statistics to see where things are going in the next 12-18.

Matt Damon Reveals Why He Passed on Starring in ‘Ted’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

'The Odyssey' star would've played John Bennett, a role that went to fellow Boston native Mark Wahlberg.

In a new interview with GQ, Damon revealed that he passed on starring in Seth MacFarlane‘s 2012 comedy as John Bennett, the best friend of the foul-mouthed, party-loving teddy bear named Ted.

“I don’t want to live with the regret, but I can tell you that I passed on Ted, and I’ve never had this experience where I laughed so hard reading a script,” The Odyssey star said. “I literally took it to dinner and was reading parts of it out to my friends at dinner. And then I just got too clever by half. I don’t know what I was thinking. I was like, Well, maybe the teddy bear’s actually the best part.…” Related Stories Movies Christopher Nolan's 'The Odyssey' Will Return to Theaters Next Month as Awards Season Heats Up Lifestyle GQ Expands Men of the Year Footprint in L.A.

Damon went on to explain why he decided to go in a different direction — and why he still thinks about what could have been.

“I overthought it and I talked myself out of it,” he said. “And because it shot in Boston, it would’ve been a chance for me to be home — my father’s passed away now, and I look back and I go, Man, that would’ve been six months back [home].”

The role ultimately went to fellow Boston native Mark Wahlberg, who stars alongside the talking teddy bear in the 2015 sequel, Ted 2.

Damon praised Wahlberg’s performance, saying the “right actor” got the part and that the movie turned out great. “I wouldn’t have made it any better,” he added.

The success of the two films later spawned a Ted prequel series on Peacock, which ran for two seasons.

Speaking with The Hollywood Reporter earlier this year, the Family Guy creator discussed the franchise’s future after the series ended and said he would be interested in making a third film.

“It’s up to Universal. It’s up to Peacock. A third season in the near future — I don’t know that there are any plans for that,” he said. “I’ve thrown the idea out that maybe we do a direct-to-Peacock Ted feature with this cast. That might be something that’s kind of fun. It was what, 10 years between the last Ted movie and the first episode of this series, and people were still there for it. He’s a character we can bring back at any time.”

What Tom Gardner Said About Days Like This

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Tom didn't predict today's drop. He did explain why it shouldn't surprise us and what Fools should do about it.

If you opened your portfolio today and winced, you're in good company. As of market close, the S&P 500 was down 4.36% and the Nasdaq 5.52%. If you own a lot of growth stocks, your account probably fell harder than that.

Back in September, Tom Gardner sat down to record his sixth Quarterly Call, and he spent a good chunk of it on days like this one. He didn't predict today. Tom doesn't make market calls, and he gave "a mild wrist slap" to people who make spectacular ones. Instead, he explained why a drop like this shouldn't catch a Fool off guard and what to do when one arrives. Here are five ideas from that call worth rereading before you touch the sell button.

Tom reminded us that the S&P 500 has historically fallen 10% about once a year. A pullback of 10% to 15%, he said, is "par for the course." Think of it as the toll you pay for decades of stock market returns.

He also argued the odds of a pullback were higher than usual when he recorded the call. The S&P 500 and Nasdaq were trading 10% to 30% above their typical historical valuation ranges. When stocks are priced for good news, it takes less bad news to knock them down.

And he warned that when the index falls, the high-growth, high-beta stocks many Fools own tend to fall further. High beta means a stock swings harder than the market in both directions. So if your portfolio is down more than the S&P 500 today, that's the trade-off Tom told us to expect.

"Emotional reactivity is the enemy of wealth creation," Tom said. A red day tempts you toward the dramatic move: Sell everything, or back up the truck.

Tom's alternative is incrementalism. Buy and sell in stages and recheck your thesis at every step. His example came from Hidden Gems, which sold 15% of its CrowdStrike (CRWD -9.88%) position in the weeks before the call. Tom said the team didn't expect calamity for the business. The stock looked richly priced, so they took some cash off the table and kept the rest.

Tom argued that moving in steps builds conviction. Each partial sale makes you rethink your reasons, and that can get you out of a bad position sooner than an all-or-nothing standoff in your head. If you feel the urge to do something today, do a small something.

Tom's first recommendation for this market was to pair higher-quality companies with lower return expectations. He used Costco (COST +1.83%) to show what he means.

He thinks Costco could deliver about 10% a year plus a small dividend. Over a decade, that turns $10,000 into roughly $27,000. Tom admitted that sounds dull to anyone who's seen a stock do 2.7 times its money in six months. So he asked members to picture Costco handing them about 10% a year for the next decade and to decide honestly whether that would satisfy them. Your answer, he said, sets the frame for how you invest from here.

The danger runs the other way. Lower-quality companies carrying high expectations can fall 50%, 70%, or 90% "and never come back," Tom warned. If one of those becomes one of your largest positions, it can be "quite damaging to your pursuit of financial freedom," he said, noting that it's a good idea to have "solid ballast" in your portfolio. Ballast is the heavy weight in a ship's hold that keeps it steady.

Tom reaffirmed a core Foolish principle: Own 50 or more companies and hold them for more than five years on average.

His reminder of why came from a stock he owns. Rocket Lab (RKLB -12.18%) had run up to $150 a share and then been cut in half by the time of the call. Tom still believes in the business, but he called that kind of swing a reminder of "the importance of having a diversified portfolio."

Tom's final action step was about your household, and it may matter most on a day like today. He suggested a family exercise. Figure out how cheaply you could live for a month and still enjoy your life. Then ask how big your emergency fund should be. Is six months enough? Nine? Twelve?

His goal is to simulate a recession, a market decline, or a job change before any of them shows up. "Taking the step to improve the sustainability of a high-quality life is vastly more significant and important than squeezing out extra percentage points of returns, particularly in a richly priced market," he said.

A household with its bills covered and cash in reserve can treat a falling market as a sale. Tom described investors who get through a 20% or even 40% decline "in style." They keep a cash position on the side and put it to work in their favorite companies at lower prices. In a reply to members in the call's comments section, Tom said his own cash position sits in the 10% to 15% range.

Tom closed the first section of his call with a line from his father, who created the Potential Growth Indicator you'll find on our site. After a long, happy day, when the Gardner kids asked to stay up late, their dad would say, "Tomorrow's another day." Tom offered that advice to investors who try to cram every win into today. It works just as well for losses. A bad day is one day, and Fools invest for decades.

Tom Gardner has positions in CrowdStrike and Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Costco Wholesale, CrowdStrike, and Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

‘SNL’ Writer Jimmy Fowlie Pays Tribute to Sister Christina Downer After Police Arrest Two for Her Murder

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

"It brings our family and her friends some relief to know that charges have been filed in connection with Christina’s death... Today I want to spend a little time remembering how much fun I had with her and how lucky I am that she was my sister," Fowlie wrote in a post for Downer's birthday.

Saturday Night Live writer Jimmy Fowlie is paying tribute to his sister, Christina Downer, on her birthday almost a week after police announced they have arrested two people for her murder.

Fowlie shared an Instagram post of himself and Downer at a movie theater in front of a promotional photo-op for Tron: Ares, which he noted was a “video of what turned out to be our last moment together.” He also thanked “everyone who’s reached out” and addressed news that Downer’s boyfriend, Delorean Bijon Brandon, and Kaylee Nicole Markowski had been charged with her murder. Related Stories TV 'SNL': Inde Navarrette Digs at Shane Gillis' Joke About Women Dressing Like "Whores" at the Gym as Tom Hanks Crashes Cold Open News LAPD Arrests Two for Murder of Christina Downer, Sister to 'SNL' Writer Jimmy Fowlie

“It brings our family and her friends some relief to know that charges have been filed in connection with Christina’s death. Because this whole experience has been so endlessly depressing, today I want to spend a little time remembering how much fun I had with her and how lucky I am that she was my sister,” he wrote at the end of the post. “Happy birthday, Chris!”

The Los Angeles Police Department announced on Oct. 30 that Brandon and Markowski had been arrested exactly on Oct. 29. “On December 18, 2025, Christina Downer was reported missing after her ex-husband requested a welfare check when he had not heard from her for an extended period of time. Downer’s boyfriend, Delorean Brandon, claimed that she had moved out several months earlier, leaving behind all her belongings, including her vehicle and dog,” the LAPD said in a statement.

In their statement, police said, “Robbery-Homicide Division detectives assumed the investigation after Downer’s cellphone was discovered washed ashore on Santa Monica Beach. Through various investigative techniques, detectives identified Delorean Brandon and Kaylee Markowski as the individuals responsible for Downer’s murder, the disposal of her body, and the concealment of their involvement.”

Elswhere in Fowlie’s tribute, he described the events that led up to he and Downer’s night at the movies captured in his Instagram video.

“It truly was just an inconsequential night at the movies. I’ve always thought those little photo ops at the Grove were so puzzling. I’d see them and try to imagine a world where me and my friends would pose in front of one non-ironically. For some reason on this day, i decided to make @nletreize take one of me and Christina as we were leaving,” he wrote. I just wanted to be extra and make her laugh, and I love how you can see her giggling as she walks up. She’s wearing her slippers which i think is so cute. In this video i feel like you can feel how sweet her energy is.”

The SNL writer continued, “She was one of my favorite people to make laugh. You could argue my whole comedy career was actually born out of sitting at the dinner table as kids and acting dumb while our dad became increasingly irate at me. It was a sacrifice that was always worth it.”

On Dec. 22, 2025, Fowlie shared a social media post asking for help finding his missing sister, sharing a multiple of images of Downer. Months later, in April of this year, he announced that “the LAPD has informed our family that Christina is no longer alive, and the case has officially transitioned from a missing person to a homicide investigation.”

The LAPD’s Robbery-Homicide Division will present the case to the L.A. Country District Attorney’s Office for filing consideration. The investigation is still ongoing.

4 Speed Records and Free Solos That Went Down in the Last 2 Weeks

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Becca Droz (left) and Kate Kelleghan (right) set a new female speed record on the classic 'Naked Edge' in Boulder's Eldorado Canyon on October 26. (Photo: COROS)

Maybe it’s the erratic weather pressures of El Niño, or perhaps there’s just a particularly competitive spirit in the air right now. In the past two weeks, several climbers have broken records, made improbable repeats, and soloed terrain that would make many of us squirm.

Last week, we looked into the culture of free solo speed climbing in Boulder, Colorado, speaking with several athletes about the risks and influences they carry in their high-stakes sport. On the heels of this deep dive, two of the athletes who spoke to us went on to break additional records of their own within the past few days. This includes a new FKT on Half Dome and a fresh women’s speed record on Eldorado’s most famous route.

More broadly, we’ve seen a few historic achievements in the past couple weeks by free soloists and speed athletes alike. Here are four record-breakers we’ve been watching lately, in chronological order.

Just a few weeks ago, Alex Honnold told Adrian Ballinger that he thought someone would likely repeat his free solo of El Capitan before anyone dared to repeat his insanely hard linkup of peaks and scrambles in Red Rock. Back in 2022, he dubbed that linkup the Honnold Ultimate Red Rock Traverse (aka the HURT).

Honnold will have to eat his words, because on October 19, Nevada climbing guide Nik Mirhashemi put down the world’s second HURT, with slight modifications. Friends have been calling his revised route the NURT: Nik’s Ultimate Red Rock Traverse.

For the NURT, the 36-year-old skipped four non-technical summits, including Goodman, Buffalo Wall, Sandstone North, and Limestone North. Instead he traversed from White Rock Peak to ascend the seven-pitch Frigid Air Buttress (5.9+; 800ft). He then continued to the top of Icebox Peak. “Basically, I had to choose between adding Frigid Air or sticking with the OG route, where those nontechnical peaks are,” Mirhashemi said. “I didn’t want to skip Frigid Air as it is quite classic.” He also called the section he swapped in one of the traverse’s “routefinding cruxes.”

Despite the variations, Mirhashemi doesn’t think there’s a significant difference in effort level. He said that though his traverse is slightly shorter, he added more technical terrain, making the difference essentially a wash. “Nice to add a little personal flair to these things I suppose,” Mirhashemi said.

“I wanted to try the HURT for similar reasons to Alex,” Mirhashemi told Climbing. “Super long days of soloing moderate terrain is one of my favorite things to do. I’ve been coming out to Red Rock for many years and just moved here a year ago. I’m always looking for new ways to challenge myself and it just made sense as a goal right in my backyard.”

Last spring, Mirhashemi made his first HURT attempt, then completed several “scoping missions”  to prepare for his fall go. “I was never sure if I could pull it off,” he said. “Stoked it’s not haunting me anymore!”

Kate Kelleghan has been busy breaking her own record again, shaving minutes and seconds off the female fastest known time on The Naked Edge (5.11b), a classic multi-pitch in Colorado’s Eldorado Canyon.

Kelleghan, along with her French partner Laura Pineau, already held the record for the route’s women’s speed record of 37 minutes and eight seconds, which was 32 seconds faster than Kelleghan’s previous go in 2021 with Becca Droz, a Boulder-based climbing guide.

In late October, Kelleghan began working the route again with Droz. And on October 26, the athletes set a new record of 36 minutes, 26 seconds, shaving 42 seconds off the previous record.

“Becca and I were the team that started the women’s record, so taking it back together feels full circle for our friendship and our community,” Kelleghan said. Droz added that being able to track splits, as well as time out the approach, actual climb, and descent with their COROS watches “was a game changer”; both women are COROS athletes.

The men’s record is held by John Ebers and Ben Wilbur at 24 minutes and 14 seconds.

Just a couple months after smashing the speed record on the First Flatiron in Boulder, 32-year-old American climber Michael Reese established a fastest known time on Yosemite’s Half Dome on October 28. In so doing, he broke an 11-year FKT set by the late Dean Potter. Reese climbed the 2,000-foot batholith from the Mist Trailhead to its summit in 1 hour, 12 minutes, and 58 seconds. He followed Potter’s original line up Grizzly Gully, then free soloed Snake Dike (5.7 R; 800ft). He broke Potter’s previous record set on May 3, 2015 by about six minutes.

A few days after his Half Dome lap, our reporter Holly Chen, who also reported on his Flatiron speed record, caught up with Reese.

“In 2021, one of Dean Potter’s good friends told me about this as a potential record to try. He became one of my main climbing partners,” Reese told Chen. Since then, Reese has made 16 attempts and several scouting missions before his record-breaking attempt in late October.

“Records are made to be broken. I’m not gonna be surprised if it’s broken one day,” Reese said.

In the second-hardest free solo by a woman, Aleksandra “Ola” Taistra climbed a seven-pitch, 720-foot route in Sardinia, Italy on October 29. The line in question is Margarites (7a+/5.12a) up the limestone cliffs of Monte Oddeu. Established in 1998 by Andrea Calvo and Lorenzo Nadali, the route has become one of Sardinia’s “most sought-after lines,” according to Taistra. It includes a bouldery 7a+/5.12a crux pitch high off the deck; two 7a/5.11d pitches; and four additional pitches graded 6c+/5.11c, 6b+/5.11a, 6a+/5.10c, and 5c/5.10a, in order of descending difficulty.

“Climbing Margarites for the first time I thought that climbing it without a rope was impossible for me,” Taistra shared with Climbing, “and that’s exactly how the plan was born—first get to know the route, and then properly assess my skills.” Taistra says that when she finished free soloing Margarites, she thought: “Oh, that’s it?”

Taistra already put down one of the hardest female free solos in history in May, when she climbed Alfredo Alfredo (5.11d/7a) ropeless. Now, the only known harder female free solo belongs to French legend Catherine Destivelle, who climbed an unnamed route in Mali without a rope that she said maxed out at 7b or 5.12a/b.

In May, 44-year-old Taistra told Climbing that free soloing gives her “so much peace” and feels less stressful than climbing with a rope. The 44-year-old climber rehearses her routes on toprope until she feels confident in the moves.

NexPoint RE Finance (NREF) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Shrinking multifamily supply and rising life science leases shape next moves.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

NexPoint Real Estate Finance, Inc. (NREF -7.17%) reported third quarter results characterized by stable dividend coverage and the tactical deployment of capital into high-yielding residential and life science credit. Management reported that the company is actively developing a supplemental capital program to address liquidity needs for sponsors of agency-underwritten multifamily debt facing extension tests. The company stated it maintains a clean capital structure with significant dry powder following a preferred stock offering and an upsized credit facility. Management indicated that the current supply-demand dynamics in multifamily, marked by a significant drop in new construction starts, are expected to restore pricing power as the pipeline bottoms in 2027.

This conference call also includes an analysis of non-GAAP financial measures. For a more complete discussion of these non-GAAP financial measures, see the company's presentation that was filed earlier today.

I would now like to turn the call over to Paul Richards. Please go ahead, Paul.

Paul Richards: Thanks, Carley, and good morning, everyone. I'll walk through our quarterly results, cover the balance sheet, and provide guidance for Q4 before turning it over to Matt for a deeper dive on the portfolio and the macro lending environment. For the third quarter, we reported a net loss of $0.65 per diluted share compared to $1.14 for Q3 2025. Earnings available for distribution was $0.52 per diluted share in Q3 compared to $0.51 per diluted share in the same period of 2025. Cash available for distribution was $0.60 per share in Q3 compared to $0.53 per diluted share in the same period of 2025.

We paid a regular dividend of $0.50 per share in the third quarter, which was 1.2x covered by the cash available for distribution. On October 26, 2026, the board declared a dividend of $0.50 per share payable for the fourth quarter of 2026. Book value per diluted share decreased 5.2% from Q2 2026 to $17.63 per diluted share, primarily driven by unrealized losses on a few of our fair valued positions due to a rapid rise in rates over the quarter, which has subsequently retraced and rallied back a substantial amount, which we believe should assist in a marked rebound for Q4. Turning to new investments during the quarter.

We have continued to originate new investments across our target asset classes, funded through a combination of retained operating cash flow, proceeds from our Series C Preferred offering, and additional capacity under our secured financing facilities, reflecting our continued ability to identify and execute attractive opportunities that drive returns for our shareholders. We funded a $4 million preferred equity investment in a multifamily property that pays a monthly coupon of 14%, a $2.2 million preferred equity investment in a marina at a 13% coupon, and funded an additional $91.7 million on other existing commitments in the quarter.

During the quarter, we upsized our $375 million term loan facility and total return swap with Mizuho Capital Markets to $450 million, which we used to fund existing commitments, primarily an $80.5 million bridge loan yielding 14% that paid off at the end of the quarter. In accordance with the payoff, we paid down $52.2 million on the secured facility, lowering total outstanding balance to $397.8 million. Combined with the $27.9 million we raised in our Series C Preferred, we head into the last quarter of 2026 with what we believe to be one of the cleanest, most flexible capital structures in the commercial mortgage REIT sector.

Our portfolio is comprised of 83 investments with a total outstanding balance of $1 billion. Our investments are allocated across sectors as follows: 40.7% life sciences, 38.3% multifamily, 14% single family rental, 4.6% storage, 2.2% industrial, and 0.2% marina. Our fixed income portfolio is allocated across investments as follows: 28.4% preferred equity investments, 22.8% mezzanine loans, 18.2% revolving credit facilities, 17.9% CMBS B-pieces, 6.4% senior loans, 3.9% IO strips, and 2.3% promissory notes.

The assets collateralizing our investments are allocated geographically as follows: 31.1% Massachusetts, 15% Texas, 7% North Carolina, 6.5% Florida, and 5.5% California, with the remainder across states with less than 5% exposure, reflecting our heavy preference for Sunbelt markets, with Massachusetts and California exposure heavily weighted towards life science. The collateral on our portfolio is 78.3% stabilized, with 64.7% loan-to-value and a weighted average DSCR of 1.39x. We have $913.1 million of debt outstanding with a weighted average cost of 5.8% and has a weighted average maturity of two and half years. Our secured debt is collateralized by $1.5 billion of collateral with a weighted average maturity of 2.6 years and a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.97x.

Moving to guidance for the fourth quarter. Earnings available for distribution, $0.42 per diluted share at the midpoint, with a range of $0.37 on the low end and $0.47 on the high end. Cash available for distribution, $0.55 per diluted share at the midpoint, with a range of $0.50 on the low end and $0.60 on the high end. With that, I would like to turn it over to Matt for a detailed discussion of the portfolio and the current market environment. Matt?

Matt McGraner: Thank you, Paul. This quarter was a solid, consistent execution. Both EAD and CAD came in ahead of our guidance. Given our positive credit profile and the improving but still challenging macro backdrop, we continue to play offense in this environment and expect opportunities for our solution-oriented capital to grow over the near-term. The Fed held its target rate at 3.75%-4% in late October, and the 10-year Treasury moved back above 5%. Higher for longer pressures borrowers who need to refinance or meet extension tests, and that is exactly where disciplined solution capital earns its pricing power. Our new investments are priced at double-digit coupons at reset basis, and therefore, we believe we are well-positioned for this type of environment.

Turning to our verticals. Residential, between multi and single-family rental, remains our largest exposure. Our multifamily credit sits predominantly behind agency-underwritten senior debt, which has historically been some of the most resilient collateral in real estate. Freddie Mac's aggregate losses have averaged about 7 basis points per year since 2009, with $432.6 million of cumulative losses on $645.8 billion of issuance through August of 2026. That is not to say there is not stress in the system, though. Trepp has Freddie's multifamily delinquency at 80 basis points of UPB in August, up from 48 basis points in August of 2024. We see this as an opportunity, however, which I will elaborate on in a minute.

On the supply side, multifamily construction starts fell more than 40% between 2023 and 2025, and new deliveries are forecasted to bottom near 327,000 units in 2027, well below the roughly 600,000-unit peak in 2024. Supply is what broke pricing power over the last two years, and the falling supply pipeline is what we expect to restore it. That said, the recovery is not linear, and in the third quarter, blended lease rates across our portfolios remained flat to slightly positive, and concessions, while abating, are still prevalent in the market for Class A and Class B multifamily in our geographical footprint.

The good news is we are underwriting and deploying our capital into new residential investments on today's rents, again, on reset basis and not on a recovery pro forma. Our single-family rental book continues to perform really well, with significant equity cushion. Overall, we view the sector's credit profile as slightly more favorable than multifamily, with rents already in positive territory and less pockets of distress. The number of households renting single-family homes continue to rise, and higher for longer should keep demand high for our affordable SFR in our markets. Now on to life science.

At LYFE, the sponsor's recap process is underway, and we continue to expect a substantial, if not all, of our return of capital in the next quarter or two. When the capital comes back, our plan is to redeploy most of it into residential collateral and to move life science back toward roughly one-third of the portfolio over time. Across the rest of our life science exposure, fundamentals continue to improve sequentially, particularly in Boston and San Francisco, with 220,000 sq ft of new leases signed during the year-to-date. The remaining industry-wide speculative pipeline is expected to deliver through 2026, after which new supply will be limited largely to fully pre-leased build-to-suit projects.

Demand is broadening beyond traditional lab users to biomanufacturing, onshoring, and AI-driven users that need power density, floor loads, and lab-grade infrastructure. Importantly, and again, most of this book was originated in 2025 through 2026 at a reset basis. In self-storage, fundamentals troughed in 2025, and with new construction pipelines well below historical averages, we expect conditions to keep strengthening. During the quarter, we continued to add small mezzanine positions on newly stabilized storage assets at double-digit coupons. The sector has already largely reported better-than-expected results during the quarter, and our NSP-owned portfolio continues to perform very well also, with occupancy as of 9/30 at 93.5% and rents up 600 basis points year-over-year.

On the pipeline in April, we outlined more than $190 million of NREF investments and more than $225 million of structured product opportunities. Year-to-date, I'm pleased to say we have closed $280 million of that $415 million pipeline and have another $160 million committed over the next 12 months with an active pipeline backlog building. We were deliberately selective in the third quarter, and with the additional Mizuho capacity and expected repayments, we have the dry powder to lean in at attractive spreads in the fourth quarter and into 2027. I'm glad we waited on some of the resi opportunities because more will appear.

One critical need for the market will be to provide solution and special situation capital for the residential sector, and this is a great opportunity for NREF over the next couple of years, with the caveat that it might not fully materialize if the capital markets rapidly improve, which we wouldn't hate either. The stress in agency multifamily is concentrated in floating rate loans originated in 2020 through 2023, when SOFR was near zero. Those loans need replacement rate caps, cap escrows, and in many cases, a paydown to meet their refi and extension test, and a meaningful number of sponsors do not have the equity to do it.

In agency floating rate securitizations alone, that vintage is more than $32 billion across roughly 1,000 loans, and about a quarter of that balance is already on special servicer watch lists. We have demonstrated time-and time again that we can be a creative partner with the agencies, particularly Freddie, to provide solution-oriented capital on a bespoke basis. We're already developing a program where NREF would provide co-terminous supplemental capital behind the existing first lien so that sponsors can buy the cap, fund reserves, and pass their tests rather than hand back the keys. This is exactly the kind of solution capital we have built the platform around.

It sits behind conservatively underwritten agency senior debt in an asset class and in markets we know as operators, and it is priced like rescue capital. We would also expect it to be financed on a matched term basis, similar to how our original SFR portfolio was financed. The program is not final, but we do believe there is a strong need for it, and we are excited about it. To close and summarize, we are pleased with where we are, particularly in this cycle. Earnings came in ahead of guidance, the dividend is covered, credit continues to hold well, and our balance sheet has more capacity than it did a year ago.

As always, I want to thank this team for their hard work and focus, and with that, we would like to turn the call over to the operator and take your questions.

Operator: We will now begin the question and answer session. If you would like to ask a question, please press star one to raise your hand. To withdraw your question, press star one again. We ask that you pick up your handset when asking a question to allow for optimum sound quality. If you are muted locally, please remember to unmute your device. Please stand by while we compile the Q&A roster. Your first question comes from the line of Jade Rahmani with KBW. Your line is open. Please go ahead.

Jade Rahmani: Thank you very much. Could you give an update on the IQHQ exposure, specifically the credit facility and preferred equity position, and how you expect that to perform over the next one to two years?

Matt McGraner: Yeah. Happy to. Good morning. The preferred and the RCF sit at different detachment points, but it's roughly, I think, $350 million collectively. The company is, as I've said in the past, executing on its plan, seeing a lot of good traffic in Boston and San Francisco in particular. The near-term, I would say, catalyst is Alewife, and they're continuing to either recap or monetize and/or sell that asset. To the extent that it's sold, then that frees up a ton of capital to delever the company and take out some of our capital.

In terms of, I'd say, the widening funnel, one thing that's popped up in the last quarter or two, these assets have not only really great infrastructure, but they unbeknownst to the sponsor has a great deal of powered shell. The new construction pref and mezz positions are doing extremely well and delivering kind of on schedule, on budget, and then hopefully into an environment in 2029 where there's going to be no new supply. And the industry needs it. It's going to need this sliver capital coming in, particularly if the Fed continues to hike interest rates into this multi-cycle. Yeah, nothing alarming at this point.

Where can you guys put money to work today, and how different is it, at least from a return perspective, for NREF relative to a year ago, two years ago, et cetera? Obviously, capital can't be too expensive or it doesn't work for anybody in the stack.

Gabriel Poggi: Just kind of give an overview of kind of where you can. Is it still kind of the same zip code of what you've been doing, a little higher, et cetera, would be helpful.

Matt McGraner: Yeah. We can underwrite to high single digits with this solution capital, and if we can do it with the agencies, then we can get back leverage on it like we have in the past, and that turns it into a mid-teens return. What I'd say is that the kind of return there, the return is still there, but what I think is now kind of coming home to roost is that the opportunities are starting to appear. Heretofore, most folks were, most banks and non-bank lenders were willing to kind of hold through the cycle and just work it out themselves and maybe take on some REO and sell it into a strengthening environment in 2026 and 2027.

With the latest hike in interest rates, the exhaustion is starting to appear, I think, on some of these borrowers and sponsors and also some of the lenders. I think that they're starting to be more open for the first time to ideas and solutions that we're bringing. I think the return's going to be there. It's not going to be anything like to the depth of life science, but it is going to be an interesting place to play on a risk-return profile, and it'll have some size. Like I said, we identified $30 billion or so of opportunities.

Operator: Just a reminder, if you'd like to ask a question, please press star one to raise your hand. There are no further questions at this time. This concludes today's call. Thank you for attending. You may now disconnect.

Meet *sshole, the vulnerability that lets hackers SSH into Linux devices

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Certain configurations of Linux are affected. Other distros may be as well.

Devices running some configurations of Alpine Linux, and possibly other distributions, are affected by a decade-old vulnerability that allows attackers to log into devices with no password.

The vulnerability exists in the interaction between OpenSSH, the code library implementing SSH connections, and musl, a lightweight alternative to the libc library that, unlike most other Linux distributions, is used in Alpine. Further, OpenSSH using musl (pronounced muscle) must not use PAM (pluggable authentication modules), a framework that handles user authentication and account-access checks. Alpine is a lightweight, widely used Linux distribution. Other platforms may be affected if they use the same configurations.

There are two other conditions necessary for successful exploitation. First, a system must have at least one user account that’s accessible using a password that is hashed using the SHA256 or SHA512 functions. Second, the target system account must be configured to be passwordless, by way of specifying a “*” in the password field in /etc/shadow, the Linux file where password hashes are stored. Passwordless accounts are commonly used for accounts that use certificate-based authentication. This setting is common for certain types of accounts.

When all the conditions are met, the consequences are high.

“An attacker who can reach your SSH port can log in without knowing any password,” Alpine maintainers said Thursday. “It takes one connection, works every time, and needs no retries.”

When logging into a passwordless account, the attacker can gain access by inputting more than 256 characters in the password field.

Security firm Checkpoint said that the use of the SHA256 or SHA512 functions “matters because sshd borrows that account’s salt. Without it, the attack fails.”  Company researchers said their testing also showed that the account must be active, not just hold a hash. Checkpoint further reported that root accounts are safe under the default PermitRootLogin prohibit password setting, though reachable accounts with doas or sudo rights can still become root.

Despite the prerequisites, *sshole—the vulnerability name given by the person who discovered it—likely threatens large numbers of systems. The Docker Hub base image for Alpine has more than 1 billion downloads. Alpine has long been popular in routers, smart TVs, industrial controllers, and other types of embedded devices, many of which can’t be easily updated, if at all. More recently, Alpine has been adopted for use in desktops and servers. Alpine is also used in many container images.

According to security firm Detectify, Shodan lists around 22 million systems with SSH open on port 22. Only a fraction meet the conditions necessary for exploitation, but given the ease and speed of testing each one, locating vulnerable devices won’t require much effort or time.

It’s not entirely clear where the vulnerability resides. OpenSSH, which issued an update Thursday, said: “We consider the root cause of this bug to be outside sshd, but it is exploitable via sshd and can be avoided there.” Musl developer Rich Felker, meanwhile, wrote: “It is my position that this is not a bug in musl just an erroneous use by the caller.” Felker went on to say that musl developers have wanted to make changes to the mechanism “for a while now” and planned to do so in an upcoming release. Alpine has also issued an update.

It’s not yet known what other packages may be vulnerable. The person who discovered *sshole said OpenWRT is affected, but by default, that OS uses the Dropbear package, not OpenSSH, for SSH connections (though maintainers provide instructions for installing the latter). The discoverer also names the packages buildroot and yocto as being affected, but that isn’t yet confirmed.

Despite the uncertainty, anyone using a Linux system that uses both musl and OpenSSH should take time to immediately investigate if they’re affected. A good starting point is here. Both Alpine and OpenSSH patches should be installed on all affected systems. There is no known CVE designation for the vulnerability.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Hudson Pacific (HPP) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Occupancy rose a fifth straight quarter as debt fell and studios held steady.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Hudson Pacific Properties, Inc. (HPP -17.05%) reported a period of active leasing and balance sheet management as it navigated shifting demand in West Coast office and studio markets. Management reported that the company prioritized debt reduction through strategic asset dispositions and the opportunistic repurchase of senior notes while maintaining a focus on high-quality assets in technology-driven submarkets. The company stated that leasing activity was primarily concentrated in the San Francisco Bay Area, supported by the ongoing recovery in the technology and venture capital sectors. Furthermore, management indicated that the studio segment showed signs of stabilization with improved production spending in New York and potential legislative catalysts in California.

Operator: Hello, everyone. Thank you for joining us, and welcome to the Hudson Pacific Properties third quarter 26 earnings call. After today's prepared remarks, we will host a question and answer session. If you would like to ask a question, please press *1 to raise your hand. To withdraw your question, press 1 again. I will now hand the conference over to Laura Campbell, executive vice president, investor relations and marketing. Laura, please go ahead.

Laura Campbell: Good morning, everyone. Thanks for joining us. With me on the call today are Victor J. Coleman, chairman and CEO, Mark T. Lammas, President; Harout Krikor Diramerian, CFO and Arthur X. Suazo, EVP of Leasing. This morning, we filed our earnings release and supplemental on an 8-K with the SEC and both are now available on our website along with an audio webcast of this call for replay. Some of the information we will share today on the call is forward looking in nature. Please reference our earnings release and supplemental for statements regarding forward looking information. As well as the reconciliation of non GAAP financial measures used on this call.

Today, Victor will focus on current industry and market trends. Mark will provide detail on our office leasing and studio operations, and Harout will review our financial results and 2026 outlook. Thereafter, we will be happy to take your questions. Victor?

We signed 534 thousand square feet of new and renewal office leases and our in service office occupancy grew for the 5th consecutive quarter approaching 86%. Together with improved studio performance, that contributed to growth of 8% in same store cash NOI and a core FFO per share year over year, and we are once again raising our full year outlook. We also continue to execute on our disposition plan and strengthen our balance sheet.

Pro forma for our fourth quarter asset sales and senior notes repurchase total liquidity stands at $840 million with our share of debt reduced by 14% year-over-year. And looking ahead, our leasing pipeline stands at 2.1 million square feet with 1.1 million square feet now in advanced stages nearly twice the level of a year ago. A pipeline of similar size set the stage for a very strong 2026. And its greater maturity today further positions us to extend our earnings growth into 2027. Touching on our markets, I am going to begin with the capital fueling demand. US venture backed companies raised $98.4 billion in the third quarter.

While below the outsized first half totals it was roughly double the quarterly levels of 2023 and 2024. Year to date investment now exceeds $515 billion already more than 60% above full-year 2025. With over 70% of it deployed in our West Coast markets. AI remains the primary driver accounting for 20 US rounds of $1 billion or more in the quarter while software also has captured a record share of venture dollars this year. Several indicators support continued funding. US VC fundraising reached over $100 billion through the third quarter already ahead of all of 2025 and a record acquisition exit values are recycling capital back into the ecosystem.

We are mindful of the recent volatility in tech stocks, which is largely centered on the capital spending of the largest platforms, that is a different part of the AI economy from the 1 driving demand in our markets. We will continue to watch how public market sentiments affect private funding, but so far, we have not seen it in our pipeline or tour activity. On the ground, demand continues to build. AI and tech driven leasing is broadening the office recovery across the West Coast with virtually no new supply coming online.

San Francisco posted its 8th consecutive quarter of positive net absorption adding 1.3 million square feet Citywide vacancy has improved 660 basis points over the past year, and asking rents are up nearly 10%. The South Financial District continues to lead having absorbed more than 2 million square feet so far this year. With large blocks in the South of Market quickly disappearing, we expect tenants to widen their searches into adjacent submarkets which should benefit our assets greatly. Silicon Valley delivered its strongest leasing quarter since 2019. At 4.1 million square feet It also absorbed more than 1 million square feet and active tenant demand rose to 12.4 million square feet with Santa Clara among the leading submarkets.

On the peninsula, net absorption turned positive led by Redwood City, Redwood Shores at more than 40% of the region's total, while neighboring Palo Alto remains 1 of the Bay Area's tightest office markets with rents among the highest in the country. Further north, in Seattle, net absorption reached its highest level since 2021. While Downtown Seattle recorded its first positive quarter in nearly 5 years on the strength of the CBD. Pioneer Square has been positive both for the quarter and the year to date. Los Angeles by contrast remains more mixed. Leasing volume rose nearly 12% year-over-year, but net absorption was modestly negative. West Los Angeles captured roughly 40% of all leases.

In Los Angeles while with Brentwood posting positive absorption this quarter. And in Vancouver, class a vacancy downtown fell for the 5th straight quarter to its lowest level since 2022 with no new downtown supply expected until at least 2032. Conditions are setting up for upward pressure on premium rents. Turning to our studio business, production levels in Los Angeles and New York continue to move in different directions. But as Mark is going to discuss, our prime locations are performing well in both. Third Quarter Los Angeles on location shoot days were 5% lower year-over-year and remained 29% below the 5-year average.

New York, meanwhile, is surging with third quarter production spending up 87% year-over-year to $1.6 billion approaching California's $1.8 billion. Filming permits in New York City were up 27% year-to-date as of September. Looking ahead, see potential catalysts for Los Angeles production At the federal level, legislation introduced this fall would create a 20% to 30% film and TV tax credit that stacks on top of the existing state credits and supporters are pushing to pass this before year end. In a bold move this week, governor Newsom proposed raising the state's annual credit from $750 million to at least $1 billion and for the first time extending eligibility to above the line compensation. Closing the gap with competing states.

This proposal will require legislative action and will likely carry into the next administration, but it is a clear signal of the state's commitment to bringing production back. We are also continuing to sharpen the portfolio through opportunistic asset sales and year to date, we closed nearly $190 million of non-core, FFO-accretive dispositions. That includes $90.5 million from the sales of 2001 Gateway and 75 and 99 Howard in the third quarter. 75.5 million of sales for the Skyport Plaza in the fourth quarter and $23 million of sales of the non core assets from Coyote across both quarters.

The office sales took largely vacant capital intensive assets off the books allowing us to focus on capital leasing at our highest potential properties. Consistent with our capital allocation priorities, we use the majority of the net proceeds to delever, which Harout is going to discuss shortly. As we refine the portfolio, its quality continues to be recognized externally. We were named as a GRESB sector leader for the 6th consecutive year and earned a 5-star rating in the 2026 real estate assessment. We also ranked first among US listed office companies for the 2nd consecutive year and 1st among all listed participants in The Americas.

That recognition reflects the investments we have made to keep our portfolio young, modern, efficient, and resilient, as well as our management team's commitment to quality and discipline. Now with that, I am going to hand it over to Mark to talk through leasing and operational details.

Driven by new and renewal leasing at our Peninsula and Silicon Valley assets. Third quarter net effective rents increased 30% year-over-year, and on a trailing 12-month basis were up 9%. And as Victor noted, more than 1 million square feet is in late stage negotiations.

As we did at 10.9 thousand Washington, we have created real optionality giving us multiple paths to respond to market demand and maximize value. Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Arsenal have added Antonio Nusa to their list of transfer targets ahead of the January window, according to new reports.

While the Gunners spent big in the summer - adding Ezri Konsa, Bruno Guimaraes and Christos Tzolis to their squad - not bringing in at least one more attacker was considered a risky strategy.

In fact, upon allowing Gabriel Martinelli, Leandro Trossard and Gabriel Jesus to leave permanently, there is an argument that Arsenal ended the summer window weaker in attack than when they started it.

As such, it has been widely reported that the club plan to prioritise signing a forward when the transfer window reopens.

The Mirror, this week, have ‘revealed’ Bournemouth’s Eli Junior Kroupi and Juventus wonderkid Kenan Yildiz are both on Mikel Arteta’s radar, though a January move for either could prove difficult.

Now, the Sun claim the Gunners are also tracking RB Leipzig winger Nusa and have been scouting the Norway international this season.

Predominantly a left-sided attacker, Nusa can also operate on the right flank and has made an impressive start to the campaign by scoring six goals and weighing in with seven assists in 14 appearances across all competitions.

The 21-year-old was on target for Leipzig in their 3-2 defeat to Manchester City in the Champions League on Wednesday night, prompting excitement among some Arsenal fans on social media as a result.

However, the aforementioned report states a deal taking Nusa to north London at the start of 2027 will be difficult to pull off as Leipzig view the winger as a key player and do not want to sell him in the middle of the season.

Furthermore, the Bundesliga club's stance his strengthened after selling Yan Diomande to Real Madrid in the summer for around £120million, meaning they are under no financial pressure to cash in on Nusa.

E-quipment highlight: Jungheinrich launches new forklift for Asian markets

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Like many equipment brands, German forklift maker Jungheinrich has been struggling to crack the Asian industrial markets – but the company is set to change all that with the launch of a new line of 1.6- to 3-ton class electric forklifts designed specifically to navigate the region’s tight warehouses and factories.

Jungheinrich launched the next generation of its EFG MB and MC electric counterbalance forklifts, giving businesses more flexibility to match truck performance with the battery options, charging systems, and operator features that meet their specific needs.

“Different operations have different requirements,” said Lisa Jing Xue, Managing Director at Jungheinrich’s Qingpu Plant, China. “With the EFG M series, customers can configure the (forklift) truck around their application, workload and operators – whether they need greater maneuverability for confined warehouse spaces, or a stable, powerful truck for demanding transport and stacking work.”

Forklift buyers can choose between two performance packages, prioritizing energy efficiency for everyday material handling or more driving and lifting muscle for more demanding, high-throughput operations. The higher-performance package comes standard on models rated for 2.5-3.0 tons, with a choice of either lead-acid or lithium-ion batteries with 346 or 690 Ah of capacity available to suit different workloads and shift patterns. Both ship with regenerative braking tech that can recover up to 30% of the energy used in stopping and lowering loads, too, helping to keep these machines running longer between charging sessions.

“We carried out extensive customer research to understand what the market needs,” added Jing Xue. “Customers can invest in the performance required today and add further capabilities as their application demands.”

Optional features include fingertip controls, a larger 4″ digital display, upgraded LED lighting, and operator-assistance (ADAS) systems, while flexible seating and cabin configurations are designed to keep drivers comfortable through long shifts. A slim B-pillar and glass roof help visibility in busy warehouses, while the four-wheel EFG MC gets automatic cornering speed reduction as standard. Integrated telematics are also standard, giving fleet managers access to utilization data, maintenance planning and pre-operational checks from day one.

Jungheinrich says the order books for both the three- and four-wheel electric forklifts are open now, ready to be supported by the company’s 6,000+ person strong service network across the Asia-Pacific region.

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

Enhanced Group Acquires Controlling Interest in German Promoter DEAG Deutsche Entertainment

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The promotion company, which operates festivals and venues across Europe, is valued at $115 million.

Enhanced Group announced Thursday (Nov. 5) that it has acquired approximately a 77% controlling interest in the Berlin-based promotion and ticketing company DEAG Deutsche Entertainment AG.

Enhanced Group will purchase its interest in the German live entertainment company for $8 per share, in a deal that values DEAG at $115 million. The new issues are subject to a lock-up period lasting for up to 12 months, with staggered release dates. Enhanced Group and DEAG expect the transaction to close in the first quarter of 2027.

“I believe one of the most significant long-term consequences of AI and robotics will be a gradual reduction in the need for human labor,” Maximilian Martin, co-founder and CEO of Enhanced, said in a statement. “And I believe people will mostly allocate this free time to shared, real life human experiences. This creates scarcity, as sports and live experiences cannot be automated or replicated by AI. A live game, a concert, a record broken in front of a crowd — all those moments create goosebumps and become more valuable as everything else becomes digitally abundant. We believe the winners will be the companies that own the content, the stage and the ticket. With DEAG, Enhanced will have all three: owned sports and live IP, a broad promoter and venue network, and proprietary ticketing. And we believe we are acquiring it at a very attractive valuation.”

According to a press release, Enhanced plans to evaluate its options for increasing ownership, “potentially up to 100%.”

DEAG has locations in Germany, the U.K., Ireland, Switzerland, Denmark, Spain and Italy, and organizes more than 6,000 live events per year, according to the company’s website. Companies operated under DEAG’s portfolio include DEAG Classics, which focuses on classical music performances; DEAG Concerts GmbH, which has worked with The Rolling Stones; and KBK, which serves as a concert and artist agency serving touring acts including Deep Purple, Lenny Kravitz and Alice Cooper, as well as promoting the W-Festival in Frankfurt, Germany.

The promoter operates festivals including Barcelona’s Rock Fest and AIRBEAT ONE Dance Festival and Indian Spirit Festival, both in Germany. It also owns three venues throughout Europe, including myticket Jahrhunderthalle in Frankfurt, Germany; Salle Métropole in Lausanne, Switzerland; and Arches London Bridge in London.

Incoming CEO Markus Breitenecker will lead DEAG, which Enhanced Group says will continue operating under the existing management team.

Additional reporting by Elizabeth Dilts Marshall.

“Oh, shut up. Just shut up. Please. Give us a break for one day and shut that ridiculous mouth,” Kimmel said. “You are such a spoiled child. Did you ever learn anything? About manners — or sportsmanship — or simple common decency? Nothing is ever your fault!”

Kimmel went on to insist that Trump resign.

“Quit already. Just quit,” he said. “Nobody wants you. Even you don’t want to be president anymore. Take that billion dollars’ worth of crypto, buy an island — maybe [Jeffrey] Epstein’s island! Wouldn’t that be a perfect end to this story?”

And the host indicated he was optimistic that a Vance presidency wouldn’t be that bad.

“And maybe you’re thinking, ‘Well, if he quit, wouldn’t that make J.D. Vance president?’ Well, yes, it would. But here’s the thing about J.D. Vance: A person who flipped from ‘Donald Trump is the American Hitler’ to ‘Donald Trump is the greatest president of my generation’ can flip back. It’s easy when you have no spine. He’s a very good flipper.”
